A procedure for constructing the directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}
.

We next describe our algorithm for constructing the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
from the directed graph
\mathscr{G}
. This algorithm is denoted by
Construct-
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathscr{M},r)
and is found in Algorithm
2
. In this algorithm, we first use Algorithm
1
to construct the directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
which is a transitive closure, and then we invoke a recursive subroutine denoted by
RecursiveStep
(\mathscr{G})
in Algorithm
3
. The goal of the recursive subroutine is to take as an input a generic directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
which is a transitive closure, and for that graph, output the collection of subsets of vertices
\mathscr{A}\equiv\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}:\text{ if $i\in S$ and $(i,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\}
. Algorithm
2
concludes by removing the subsets of vertices from
\mathscr{A}
which do not include the no-purchase option
0
, and then outputs the remaining subsets of vertices from
\mathscr{A}
. The correctness of Algorithm
2
follows immediately from our earlier observation that an assortment satisfies
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
if and only if [
0\in S
] and [we have that
i\in S
whenever there exists a product
i^{*}\in S
and a directed edge
(i^{*},i)\in\mathscr{E}
].

At a high level, the recursive subroutine in Algorithm
3
is comprised of two cases. The base case of the subroutine is when the graph has no vertices, in which case it is clear that
\mathscr{A}=\{\emptyset\}
. If we are not in the base case, then the aim of the recursive subroutine is to construct the collections
\left\{S\in\mathscr{A}:i\notin S\right\}
and
\left\{S\in\mathscr{A}:i\in S\right\}
for a chosen vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}
and output the union of these two collections. The construction of the collection
\left\{S\in\mathscr{A}:i\notin S\right\}
takes place on lines (
2b
)-(
2c
) of Algorithm
3
, and the construction of the collection
\left\{S\in\mathscr{A}:i\in S\right\}
takes place on lines (
2d
)-(
2f
) of Algorithm
3
.

Up to this point, we have established that Algorithm
1
is correct (that is, it delivers the desired output for any valid input), and we have established that Algorithm
2
is correct under the assumption that Algorithm
3
is correct. Therefore, it remains for us to prove that the recursive subroutine in Algorithm
3
is correct. To prove the correctness of the recursive subroutine, we will make use of four intermediary claims, which are denoted below by Claims
2
-
5
. The purpose of the first two intermediary claims, denoted by Claims
2
and
3
, is to show that the graphs
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime\prime})
constructed on lines (
2b
) and (
2d
) of Algorithm
3
are directed acyclic graphs that are transitive closures, which implies that
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime}
are valid inputs on lines (
2c
) and (
2e
) of Algorithm
3
. The purpose of the second two intermediary claims, denoted by Claims
4
and
5
, is to show that the union of the two collections
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}
constructed on lines (
2c
) and (
2f
) of Algorithm
3
provides the desired output on line (
2g
) of Algorithm
3
.

Construct-
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(\mathscr{M},r)

Inputs
:

•

The collection of past assortments,
\mathscr{M}\equiv\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{M}\}
.

•

The revenues of the products,
r\equiv(r_{0},r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
.

Output
:

•

The collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
corresponding to the collection of past assortments
\mathscr{M}
and the revenues
r
.

Procedure
:

1.

Construct the directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}\leftarrow\textsc{Construct-${\mathscr{G}}(\mathscr{M},r)$}
.

2.

Compute the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\leftarrow\textsc{RecursiveStep$(\mathscr{G})$}
.

3.

For each
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
:

(a)

If
0\notin S
:

i.

Update
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\setminus\{S\}
.

4.

Output
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and terminate.

Algorithm 2:
A procedure for constructing
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
.

RecursiveStep
(\mathscr{G})

Inputs
:

•

A directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
which is a transitive closure.

Output
:

•

The collection
\mathscr{A}\equiv\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}:\text{ if $i\in S$ and $(i,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\}
.

Procedure
:

1.

If
\mathscr{V}=\emptyset
:

(a)

Output the collection
\mathscr{A}\equiv\{\emptyset\}
and terminate.

2.

Otherwise:

(a)

Choose any vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}
.

(b)

Create a copy of
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
in which the vertices
\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\}
and the incoming and outgoing edges of these vertices are removed. Denote this new graph by
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
.

(c)

Compute the collection
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\leftarrow\textsc{RecursiveStep$(\mathscr{G}^{\prime})$}
.

(d)

Create a copy of
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
in which the vertices
\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\}
and the incoming and outgoing edges of these vertices are removed. Denote this new graph by
\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime\prime})
.

(e)

Compute the collection
\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\textsc{RecursiveStep$(\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime})$}
.

(f)

Compute the collection
\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}\leftarrow\{S\cup\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\}:S\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime}\}
.

(g)

Output the collection
\mathscr{A}\equiv\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\cup\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and terminate.

Algorithm 3:
A recursive subroutine which is used in Algorithm
2
.

Claim 2

Let
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. For notational convenience, let
\mathscr{E}_{i}
denote the set of incoming and outgoing edges from each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}
. Then for each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}
, we have that
\tilde{\mathscr{G}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}\setminus\{i\},\mathscr{E}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{i})
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure.

Proof D.1

Proof of Claim
2
. Let
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure, and let
i\in\mathscr{V}
be any chosen vertex. It is clear that removing a vertex and its associated incoming and outgoing edges from a directed acyclic graph will not induce any cycles. Therefore, it follows from the fact that the original graph
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
is a directed acyclic graph that the new graph
\tilde{\mathscr{G}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}\setminus\{i\},\mathscr{E}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{i})
is also a directed acyclic graph. Moreover, consider any two arbitrary vertices
j,k\in\mathscr{V}\setminus\{i\}
which satisfy the property that vertex
k
is reachable from vertex
j
in the new graph
\tilde{\mathscr{G}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}\setminus\{i\},\mathscr{E}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{i})
. Then it follows immediately from the construction of the new graph that vertex
k
is reachable from vertex
j
in the original graph
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
. Since the original graph
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
is a transitive closure, there must exist a directed edge from vertex
j
to vertex
k
in the original graph. Since neither
j
nor
k
are equal to
i
, we have thus shown that there is a directed edge
(j,k)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{i}
. Since the two vertices
j,k\in\mathscr{V}\setminus\{i\}
were chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that the new graph is also a transitive closure. Our proof of Claim
2
is thus complete.

\halmos

Claim 3

Let
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. For notational convenience, let
\mathscr{E}_{i}
denote the set of incoming and outgoing edges from each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}
. Then for each subset of vertices
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathscr{V}
, we have that
\tilde{\mathscr{G}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}\setminus\mathscr{B},\mathscr{E}\setminus(\cup_{i\in\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{E}_{i}))
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure.

Proof D.2

Proof of Claim
3
. Let
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure, let
\mathscr{B}\subseteq\mathscr{V}
be a subset of vertices, and define the directed graph
\tilde{\mathscr{G}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}\setminus\mathscr{B},\mathscr{E}\setminus(\cup_{i\in\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{E}_{i}))
. The rest of the proof follows a straightforward induction argument. Indeed, let the vertices of
\mathscr{B}
be indexed by
\mathscr{B}\equiv\{i^{\mathscr{B}}_{1},\ldots,i^{\mathscr{B}}_{|\mathscr{B}|}\}
. For each
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,|\mathscr{B}|\}
, we define the following directed graph:

\displaystyle{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}\equiv(\underbrace{\mathscr{V}\setminus\{i^{\mathscr{B}}_{1},\ldots,i^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}\}}_{\mathscr{V}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}},\;\underbrace{\mathscr{E}\setminus\left\{(k,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}:k\in\{i^{\mathscr{B}}_{1},\ldots,i^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}\}\textnormal{ or }\ell\in\{i^{\mathscr{B}}_{1},\ldots,i^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}\}\right\}}_{\mathscr{E}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}}).

We readily observe from the above definition that
{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{0}=\mathscr{G}
and that
{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{|\mathscr{B}|}=\tilde{\mathscr{G}}
. In the remainder, we will prove by induction that each
\tilde{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{0},\ldots,\tilde{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{|\mathscr{B}|}
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. Our induction proof proceeds as follows. In the base case, it follows from the equality
{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{0}=\mathscr{G}
that
{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{0}
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. Next, assume by induction that
\tilde{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{0},\ldots,\tilde{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j-1}
are directed acyclic graphs that are transitive closures for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathscr{B}|\}
. Then we readily observe from the definition of the directed graph
\mathscr{G}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j},\mathscr{E}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j})
that the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{V}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j-1}\setminus\{i^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}\};

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j-1}\setminus\left\{(k,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j-1}:\;k=i^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}\textnormal{ or }\ell=i^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}\right\}.

Using the above equalities, it follows immediately from Claim
2
and the induction hypothesis that
\mathscr{G}^{\mathscr{B}}_{j}
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. This concludes our induction proof, and since the equality
{\mathscr{G}}^{\mathscr{B}}_{|\mathscr{B}|}=\tilde{\mathscr{G}}
holds, our proof of Claim
3
is complete.
\halmos

Claim 4

Let
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. For notational convenience, let
\mathscr{E}_{i}
denote the set of incoming and outgoing edges from each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}
. For each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}
,

\displaystyle\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus\{i\}:\textnormal{ if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\}):\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus\left(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}\right),\textnormal{ then }j\in S\right\}.

(14)

Proof D.3

Proof of Claim
4
.
Let
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure, and let
i\in\mathscr{V}
be any chosen vertex from this graph. We first observe that

\displaystyle\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus\{i\}:\text{ if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\right\}

(15)

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\}):\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E},\textnormal{ then }j\in S\right\},

(16)

where the above
equality follows from the fact that
any subset of vertices
S
from the collection on line (
15
) must not contain any vertices in the graph that have an outgoing edge to vertex
i
. For notational convenience, let the collection of subsets of vertices on line (
14
) be denoted by
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
, and let the collection of subsets of vertices on line (
16
) be denoted by
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
. In other words, let

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\}):\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E},\textnormal{ then }j\in S\right\}

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathscr{A}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\}):\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus\left(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}\right),\textnormal{ then }j\in S\right\}.

In the remainder of the proof, we will show that
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}=\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
.

To show the first direction, consider any arbitrary subset of vertices
S\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
. For this subset of vertices, consider any two arbitrary vertices
k,j\in\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\})
which satisfy the conditions [
k\in S
] and [
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell})
]. In this case, it follows immediately from the fact that
\mathscr{E}\setminus(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell})\subseteq\mathscr{E}
that the condition [
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}
] holds. Therefore, it follows immediately from the definition of
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
that
j\in S
. Since the two vertices
k,j\in\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\})
which satisfy the conditions [
k\in S
] and [
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell})
] were chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that
S\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
, and since the subset of vertices
S\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
was chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
. Our proof of the first direction is thus complete.

To show the other direction, consider any arbitrary subset of vertices
S\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
. For this subset of vertices, consider any two arbitrary vertices
k,j\in\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\})
which satisfy the conditions [
k\in S
] and [
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}
]. We now suppose, for the sake of developing a contradiction, that the directed edge from vertex
k
to vertex
j
satisfies
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}
. Under this supposition, we have two cases to consider. In the first case, the directed edge satisfies
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}_{i}
. However, that would imply that either
j=i
or
k=i
, and those equalities contradict the facts that
k\in S
and
S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\})
. In the second case, the directed edge satisfies
(k,j)\in\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}
, which implies that there are directed edges
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}
and
(j,i)\in\mathscr{E}
in the graph
\mathscr{G}
. Since the graph is a transitive closure, the existence of directed edges
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}
and
(j,i)\in\mathscr{E}
implies that there must exist a directed edge
(k,i)\in\mathscr{E}
. However, the existence of a directed edge
(k,i)\in\mathscr{E}
contradicts the facts that
k\in S
and
S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\})
. In all cases, we have proved by contradiction that the vertices
k
and
j
must satisfy the condition [
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell})
]. Since the two vertices
k,j\in\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\})
which satisfy the conditions [
k\in S
] and [
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}
] were chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that
S\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
, and since the subset of vertices
S\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
was chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\subseteq\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
. Our proof of the second direction is thus complete.

We have thus shown that
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}=\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
, which concludes our proof of Claim
4
.
\halmos

Claim 5

Let
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. For notational convenience, let
\mathscr{E}_{i}
denote the set of incoming and outgoing edges from each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}
. For each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}
,

\displaystyle\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}:\left[i\in S\right]\textnormal{ and }\left[\textnormal{if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &S^{\prime}\cup\{i\}\cup\left\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\right\}:\\
&\quad S^{\prime}\in\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus\left(\{i\}\cup\left\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\right\}\right):\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus\left(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}\right),\textnormal{ then }j\in S\right\}\end{aligned}\right\}.

(17)

Proof D.4

Proof of Claim
5
.
Let
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure, and let
i\in\mathscr{V}
be any chosen vertex from this graph. Then we observe that

\displaystyle\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}:\left[i\in S\right]\textnormal{ and }\left[\textnormal{if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\right]\right\}

(18)

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}:\left[\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\}\subseteq S\right]\textnormal{ and }\left[\textnormal{if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\right]\right\}

(19)

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}:\left[\{i\}\cup\left\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\right\}\subseteq S\right]\textnormal{ and }\left[\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus\left(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}\right),\textnormal{ then }j\in S\right]\right\}.

(20)

Indeed, the equality of the collections of subsets of vertices on lines (
18
) and (
19
) follows from the fact that
any subset of vertices
S
in the collection on line (
18
) satisfies
i\in S
, and so it must contain all of the vertices in the graph which have an incoming edge from vertex
i
.
For notational convenience, let the collections of subsets of vertices on lines (
19
) and (
20
) be denoted by
\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
and
\bar{\mathscr{A}}
, respectively. To show that line (
19
) is equal to line (
20
), we first observe that the inclusion
\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}\subseteq\bar{\mathscr{A}}
follows immediately from the definitions of the collections
\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
and
\bar{\mathscr{A}}
. To show the other direction, consider any subset of vertices
S\in\bar{\mathscr{A}}
and consider any vertex
k\in S
. If there exists a directed edge
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}
which satisfies
(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}
, then we observe that the vertex
j
must satisfy
j\in\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\}
. Since it follows from
S\in\bar{\mathscr{A}}
that the inclusion
\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\}\subseteq S
holds, we conclude that
S\in\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
. We have thus shown that line (
19
) is equal to line (
20
). Since the equivalence of lines (
20
) and (
17
) follows readily from algebra, our proof of Claim
5
is complete.
\halmos

Using the above intermediary claims, we now prove the correctness of the recursive subroutine in Algorithm
3
. Indeed, it is clear that the recursive subroutine yields the correct output in the base case where
\mathscr{V}=\emptyset
. Next, let us assume by induction that the recursive subroutine yields the correct output for all valid input graphs with up to
p-1
vertices, and consider any valid input
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
for which the number of vertices is
|\mathscr{V}|=p
. Let
i\in\mathscr{V}
denote the vertex from this graph which is chosen in line (
2a
) of Algorithm
3
, where the existence of such a vertex follows from the fact that we are in the case where
\mathscr{V}\neq\emptyset
. It follows immediately from Claim
3
that the graphs
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime\prime})
constructed on lines (
2b
) and (
2d
) of Algorithm
3
are directed acyclic graphs which are transitive closures, which implies that these graphs are valid inputs to Algorithm
3
in lines (
2c
) and (
2e
). Therefore, it follows from the induction hypothesis and lines (
2c
), (
2e
), and (
2f
) of Algorithm
3
that

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\}):\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus\left(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}\right),\textnormal{ then }j\in S\right\}

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus(\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\}):\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}\setminus\left(\mathscr{E}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}\right),\textnormal{ then }j\in S\right\}

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\cup\{i\}\cup\left\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\right\}:S\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime}\right\},

where the induction hypothesis can be applied because
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime\prime})
are valid inputs to Algorithm
3
and because
|\mathscr{V}^{\prime}|\leq p-1
and
|\mathscr{V}^{\prime\prime}|\leq p-1
. Therefore, it follows from Claims
4
and
5
that

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}\setminus\{i\}:\textnormal{ if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}:\left[i\in S\right]\textnormal{ and }\left[\textnormal{if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\right]\right\},

which proves that the output of Algorithm
3
is

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\cup\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}:\textnormal{ if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}$, then $j\in S$}\right\}.

This completes our proof of the correctness of Algorithm
3
.

To conclude our proof of Lemma
4.6
, we analyze the computation time of Algorithm
2
. Indeed, we recall that the computation time required for line (
1
) in Algorithm
2
is
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}M)
. In what follows, we assume that all directed graphs are stored as adjacency lists. Under this assumption, our analysis of the computation time for line (
2
) in Algorithm
3
is split into the following two intermediary claims, denoted by Claim
6
and
7
. In our first intermediary claim, presented below as Claim
6
, we establish the computation time required for lines (
2b
), (
2d
), (
2f
), and (
2g
) of Algorithm
3
.

Claim 6

If
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure with
|\mathscr{V}|\geq 1
, then lines (
2b
), (
2d
), (
2f
), and (
2g
) of Algorithm
3
can be performed in
\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathscr{V}|\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|\right)
time.

Proof D.5

Proof.
We observe that the set of vertices
\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\}
in line (
2b
) of Algorithm
3
can be queried and stored as a hash table in
\mathcal{O}(|\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}\}|)=\mathcal{O}(|\mathscr{V}|)
computation time. Therefore, the directed graph
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
in line (
2b
) of Algorithm
3
can be constructed from scratch in
\mathcal{O}(|\mathscr{V}|+|\mathscr{E}|)
computation time. By identical reasoning, we observe that the directed graph
\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\prime\prime},\mathscr{E}^{\prime\prime})
in line (
2d
) of Algorithm
3
can be constructed in
\mathcal{O}(|\mathscr{V}|+|\mathscr{E}|)
computation time. It is easy to see for each
S\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime}
that
S\cup\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\}
is the union of three disjoint sets, which implies that the collection on line (
2f
) of Algorithm
3
can be constructed in a total of
\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{S\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime}}(|S|+|\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}\}|)\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{S\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}}|S|\right)
computation time. It is similarly easy to see that
\mathscr{A}\equiv\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\cup\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is the union of two disjoint collections, which implies that line (
2g
) of Algorithm
3
can be performed in
\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{S\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}}|S|+\sum_{S\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}}|S|\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{S\in\mathscr{A}}|S|\right)
computation time. All combined, we have shown that the computation time required for lines (
2b
), (
2d
), (
2f
), and (
2g
) of Algorithm
3
is

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathcal{O}(|\mathscr{V}|+|\mathscr{E}|)}_{\eqref{step:construct_A1:1}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{O}(|\mathscr{V}|+|\mathscr{E}|)}_{\eqref{step:construct_A2:1}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{S\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}}|S|\right)}_{\eqref{step:construct_A2:3}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{S\in\mathscr{A}}|S|\right)}_{\eqref{step:construct_A}}=\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathscr{V}|+|\mathscr{E}|+\sum_{S\in\mathscr{A}}|S|\right).

Using the fact that graphs always satisfy the inequality
|\mathscr{E}|\leq|\mathscr{V}|^{2}
and the fact that
|S|\leq|\mathscr{V}|
for all
S\in\mathscr{A}
, the above computation time simplifies to

\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathscr{V}|^{2}+\left|\mathscr{V}\right|\times\left|\mathscr{A}\right|\right).

Since
\mathscr{A}\equiv\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})
is the output of Algorithm
3
, and since it is easy to see that
|\mathscr{A}|\geq|\mathscr{V}|
, our proof of Claim
6
is complete.

\halmos

In our second intermediary claim, presented below as Claim
7
, we use Claim
6
to establish the computation time for Algorithm
3
for any valid input.

Claim 7

If
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure with
|\mathscr{V}|\geq 1
, then the computation time for Algorithm
3
is
\mathcal{O}(|\mathscr{V}|^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|)
.

Proof D.6

Proof.
Let
T(\mathscr{G})
denote the computation time required to run Algorithm
3
for any valid input
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
. It follows from Claim
6
that
T(\mathscr{G})
can be represented by an asymptotic recurrence of the form

\displaystyle T(\mathscr{G})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}|\mathscr{V}|\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|+T(\mathscr{G}^{\prime})+T(\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime}),&\text{if }\mathscr{V}\neq\emptyset,\\
1,&\text{if }\mathscr{V}=\emptyset,\end{cases}

(21)

where
\mathscr{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime}
are the subgraphs constructed in Algorithm
3
on lines (
2b
) and (
2d
). We will now prove that the above recurrence satisfies the following inequality:

\displaystyle T(\mathscr{G})\leq(|\mathscr{V}|+1)^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|.

Indeed, the above inequality clearly holds in the base case. Now assume by induction that the above inequality holds for all valid inputs graphs to Algorithm
3
with up to
p-1
vertices, and consider any valid input
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
to Algorithm
3
for which the number of vertices is
|\mathscr{V}|=p
. For this input, we have

\displaystyle T(\mathscr{G})

\displaystyle=|\mathscr{V}|\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|+T(\mathscr{G}^{\prime})+T(\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\leq|\mathscr{V}|\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|+(|\mathscr{V}^{\prime}|+1)^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G}^{\prime})\right|+(|\mathscr{V}^{\prime\prime}|+1)^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathscr{V}|\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|+|\mathscr{V}|^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G}^{\prime})\right|+|\mathscr{V}|^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime})\right|

\displaystyle=|\mathscr{V}|\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|+|\mathscr{V}|^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|

\displaystyle\leq(|\mathscr{V}|+1)^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|,

where the first line follows from (
21
) and from the fact that
\mathscr{V}\neq\emptyset
, the second line follows from the induction hypothesis, the third line follows from the facts that
|\mathscr{V}^{\prime}|<|\mathscr{V}|
and
|\mathscr{V}^{\prime\prime}|<|\mathscr{V}|
, the fourth line follows from the fact that
\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|=\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G}^{\prime})\right|+\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G}^{\prime\prime})\right|
, and the final line follows from algebra. This concludes our proof of Claim
7
.

\halmos

Our analysis of the computation time for line (
2
) of Algorithm
2
follows readily from Claim
7
. Indeed, we observe that the graph
\mathscr{G}\equiv(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{E})
that was constructed on line (
1
) of Algorithm
2
satisfies
|\mathscr{V}|=n+1
and
|\mathscr{E}|\leq(n+1)^{2}
. Therefore, it follows from Claim
7
that line (
2
) of Algorithm
2
requires
\mathcal{O}((n+1)^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep}(\mathscr{G})\right|)=\mathcal{O}(n^{2}|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|)
computation time. Since the loop on line (
3
) of Algorithm
2
can be performed in
\mathcal{O}(\sum_{S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}|S|)=\mathcal{O}(n|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|)
computation time, we have shown that the total computation time for Algorithm
2
is

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathcal{O}(n^{2}M)}_{\eqref{step:construct_S:1}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{O}(n^{2}|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|)}_{\eqref{step:construct_S:2}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{O}(n|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|)}_{\eqref{step:construct_S:3}}=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}(M+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|)\right).

This concludes our proof of Lemma
4.6
.
\halmos

Appendix E
Proof of Technical Results from §
4.3

There are two technical results in §
4.3
: Lemma
4.8
and Theorem
4.9
. Lemma
4.8
follows immediately from the definition of the collection of assortments
\widehat{S}
from §
4
, and so the proof of Lemma
4.8
is omitted. The remainder of this appendix thus contains the proof of Theorem
4.9
, which is split into several steps. We will begin by choosing any arbitrary
\bar{S}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and, for that assortment, we will define a particular realization of the historical data
v\equiv(v_{m,i}:m\in\mathcal{M},i\in S_{m})
. We will then prove in Lemma
E.1
in Appendix
E.2
for that realization of the historical data that the corresponding set of feasible solutions for the linear optimization problem (
2
) is a singleton. By showing that the realization of the historical data generates a unique feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
), we conclude in Appendix
E.3
by showing that
\bar{S}
is the unique optimal solution of the robust optimization problem (
RO
).

E.1
Assumptions and Notation

Following the statement of Theorem
4.9
, we will assume throughout Appendix
E
that
\mathscr{M}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
, that
\eta=0
, and that the revenues
r_{1}<\cdots<r_{n}
are fixed. We observe that Theorem
4.9
trivially holds in the case where
n=1
; therefore, we assume throughout the rest of Appendix
E
that the number of products satisfies
n\geq 2
.
17
17

17

Suppose the number of products satisfies
n=1
. In that case, we observe that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}=\{\{0,1\}\}
. Since
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is a singleton, Theorem
4.9
follows immediately from Theorem
4.1
.

We will make use of the following notation and preliminary results throughout Appendix
E
. First, it follows from the fact that
\mathscr{M}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
and from Definition
4.11
that the set of tuples
\mathcal{L}
can be represented compactly as
18
18

18

A formal proof of the equality on line (
22
) can be found as Lemma
5.8
in §
5.3
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\left\{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}):i_{1}\in\{0,n\}\text{ and }i_{j+1}\in\{j,i_{j}\}\text{ for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\right\}.

(22)

Second, for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, we define the indices

\displaystyle\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{1}=\cdots=\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{j}=0,\quad\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{j+1}=\cdots=\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{n}=j,

\displaystyle\bar{i}^{j}_{1}=\cdots=\bar{i}^{j}_{j}=n,\quad\bar{i}^{j}_{j+1}=\cdots=\bar{i}^{j}_{n}=j,

and we denote the tuples constructed from those indices by

\displaystyle\left(\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{n}\right)\triangleq(0,\ldots,0,j,\ldots,j)\textnormal{ and }\left(\bar{i}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}^{j}_{n}\right)\triangleq(n,\ldots,n,j,\ldots,j).

Finally, it follows from the fact that
\eta=0
that the constraints of the linear optimization problem (
2
) can be written compactly as

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{m}=i}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

(23)

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}=1

\displaystyle\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L},

where we restate for the sake of convenience that
S_{m}\equiv\{0,1,\ldots,m-1,n\}
and
\mathcal{M}\equiv\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

E.2
Construction of historical data for any given
\bar{S}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, and proof that the corresponding system (
23
) has a unique solution

In view of the strategy outlined at the beginning of Appendix
E
, we now present the details of the proof of Theorem
4.9
. Indeed, consider any arbitrary assortment
\bar{S}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
. For that assortment, we define the realization of the historical data
v_{m,i}
for each past assortment
m\in\mathcal{M}\equiv\{1,\ldots,n\}
and each product
i\in S_{m}
as follows:

\displaystyle v_{m,i}\triangleq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n}\left(1+\left|\left\{m,\ldots,n-1\right\}\setminus\bar{S}\right|\right),&\text{if }i=n,\\
\frac{1}{n}\left|\left\{m,\ldots,n-1\right\}\cap\bar{S}\right|,&\text{if }i=0,\\
\frac{1}{n},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(24)

With the realization of the historical data from line (
24
) corresponding to the assortment
\bar{S}
, the remainder of Appendix
E.2
is dedicated to proving the following lemma:

Lemma E.1

The system (
23
) is nonempty and has a unique solution
\lambda
which satisfies the following equality for each
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n},&\textnormal{if there exists }j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}\textnormal{ such that }(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})=\left(\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{n}\right),\\
\frac{1}{n},&\textnormal{if there exists }j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}\textnormal{ such that }(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})=\left(\bar{i}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}^{j}_{n}\right),\\
\frac{1}{n},&\textnormal{if }(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})=(n,\ldots,n),\\
0,&\textnormal{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(25)

Stated in words, the above Lemma
E.1
establishes that the vector
\lambda
defined by line (
25
) is the unique feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
) under the assumptions that the past assortments are
\mathscr{M}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
, that
\eta=0
, and that the realization of the historical data satisfies (
24
).

Let us provide an interpretation of the vector
\lambda
defined by line (
25
). Speaking informally, the vector
\lambda
is comprised of three types of customers. The first type of customers are denoted by the tuples
\left(\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{n}\right)
for each
j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}
, and these are customers that prefer product
j
and, if that product is unavailable, will not make a purchase. The second type of customers are denoted by the tuples
\left(\bar{i}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}^{j}_{n}\right)
for each
j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}
, and these are customers whose most preferred product is
j
, whose second-most preferred product is
n
, and will not purchase a product if neither
j
nor
n
are available. The third type of customers are denoted by the tuple
(n,\ldots,n)
, and these are customers that will purchase product
n
if it is available, and will otherwise not make a purchase. In Appendix
E.3
, we will use Lemma
E.1
to prove Theorem
4.9
.

Our proof of Lemma
E.1
makes use of two intermediary claims, denoted below as Claims
8
and
9
. These intermediary claims establish that any feasible solution of the system (
23
) must satisfy certain structural properties. We first present these two intermediary claims, followed by the proof of Lemma
E.1
.

Claim 8

Suppose that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
), and let
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
. If
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}>0
, then either
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})=(n,\ldots,n)
or there exists
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
i_{j+1}=j
.

Proof E.2

Proof of Claim
8
.
Suppose that
\lambda
is a solution of the system (
23
), and let
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
.

We begin by showing that if
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}>0
, then either
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})=(n,\ldots,n)
or there exists
m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
i_{m}\notin\{0,n\}
. We will show this claim by proving its contraposition. Indeed,
suppose that
i_{m}\in\{0,n\}
for all
m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and that there exists
\bar{m}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
i_{\bar{m}}=0
. In this case, it follows from (
22
) and from the fact that
i_{m}\in\{0,n\}
for all
m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
that
i_{1}=\cdots=i_{n}=0
. If
i_{1}=\cdots=i_{n}=0
, then

\displaystyle\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}=\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{n}^{\prime}=0}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}=v_{n,0}=0,

where the first equality follows from (
22
), the second equality follows from the fact that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
), and the third equality follows from (
24
). We have thus shown that if
i_{m}\in\{0,n\}
for all
m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and if there exists
m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
i_{m}=0
, then
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}=0
. Note that the contrapositive of this statement is that if
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}>0
, then either
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})=(n,\ldots,n)
or there exists
m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
i_{m}\notin\{0,n\}
.

We conclude the proof of Claim
8
by showing that if there exists
m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
i_{m}\notin\{0,n\}
, then there exists
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
i_{j+1}=j
. Indeed, suppose there exists
m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
i_{m}\notin\{0,n\}
. Since the inclusion
i_{1}\in\{0,n\}
always holds, the existence of
m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
satisfying
i_{m}\notin\{0,n\}
implies that there must exist
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
i_{j}\neq i_{j+1}
. Since we have assumed that the inclusion
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
holds, it follows from (
22
) and from the fact that
i_{j}\neq i_{j+1}
that
i_{j+1}
must be equal to
j
. Our proof of Claim
8
is thus complete.
\halmos

Claim 9

Suppose that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
), and let
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
. If
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}>0
and if
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\neq(n,\ldots,n)
, then there exists
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and
\dagger\in\{0,n\}
such that
i_{1}=\cdots=i_{j}=\dagger
and
i_{j+1}=\cdots=i_{n}=j
.

Proof E.3

Proof of Claim
9
.

Suppose that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
), and suppose that the inequality
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}>0
holds for a given tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
with
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\neq(n,\ldots,n)
. Then it follows from Claim
8
that there exists a product
j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
such that
i_{j^{\prime}+1}=j^{\prime}
. From this point onward, we let the product
j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
with the smallest index that satisfies
i_{j^{\prime}+1}=j^{\prime}
be denoted by

\displaystyle j\triangleq\min\left\{j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}:i_{j^{\prime}+1}=j^{\prime}\right\}=\min\left\{m\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}:i_{m+1}\notin\{0,n\}\right\},

(26)

where the last equality follows from (
22
).

For the product
j
defined in (
26
), it follows from the fact that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
) that the following two equalities must hold:

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{j+1}^{\prime}=j}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}=v_{j+1,j};\quad\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{n}^{\prime}=j}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}=v_{n,j}.

(27)

We observe from (
24
) and from the inclusion
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
that the equality
v_{j+1,j}=v_{n,j}
also holds. Therefore, (
27
) implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{j+1}^{\prime}=j}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}-\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{n}^{\prime}=j}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}=0.

(28)

Moreover, we observe from (
22
) and from the inclusion
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
that a tuple
(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}
satisfies the equality
i_{n}^{\prime}=j
if and only if
i_{j+1}^{\prime}=\cdots=i_{n}^{\prime}=j
. Therefore, (
28
) implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}:\;\begin{subarray}{c}i_{j+1}^{\prime}=j\text{ and }i_{k}^{\prime}\neq i_{k+1}^{\prime}\text{ for }\\
\text{some }k\in\{j+1,\ldots,n-1\}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}=0.

(29)

Since
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
), and since (
23
) implies that the inequality
\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}\geq 0
holds for all
(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}
, it follows from (
29
) that

\displaystyle\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}=0\quad\forall(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}\textnormal{ such that }i_{j+1}^{\prime}=j\text{ and }i_{k}^{\prime}\neq i_{k+1}^{\prime}\text{ for some }k\in\{j+1,\ldots,n-1\}.

(30)

Since we supposed that
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}>0
, and since the tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
satisfied the equality
i_{j+1}=j
, (
30
) proves that the equalities
i_{j+1}=\cdots=i_{n}=j
must hold. Moreover, it follows from the right-most equality in (
26
) that
i_{1},\ldots,i_{j}\in\{0,n\}
, which together with (
22
) implies that
i_{1}=\cdots=i_{j}
. Since (
22
) implies that
i_{1}\in\{0,n\}
, our proof of Claim
9
is complete.

\halmos

In view of Claims
8
and
9
, we now present the proof of Lemma
E.1
.

Proof E.4

Proof of Lemma
E.1
.

We begin by showing that if
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
), then
\lambda
must satisfy the equality on line (
25
) for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
.
Indeed, suppose that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
). In Claim
9
, we showed that if
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
) and if
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}>0
for some tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
where
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\neq(n,\ldots,n)
, then the tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})
must be contained in

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{*}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\textnormal{ such that }i_{1}^{\prime}=\cdots=i_{j}^{\prime}=0\textnormal{ and }i_{j+1}^{\prime}=\cdots=i_{n}^{\prime}=j\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\left\{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\textnormal{ such that }i_{1}^{\prime}=\cdots=i_{j}^{\prime}=n\textnormal{ and }i_{j+1}^{\prime}=\cdots=i_{n}^{\prime}=j\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{1}_{1},\ldots,\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{1}_{n}\right),\ldots,\left(\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{n-1}_{1},\ldots,\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{n-1}_{1}\right),\left(\bar{i}^{1}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}^{1}_{n}\right),\ldots,\left(\bar{i}^{n-1}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}^{n-1}_{1}\right)\right\},

where the notation
\left(\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{n}\right)\triangleq(0,\ldots,0,j,\ldots,j)
and
\left(\bar{i}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}^{j}_{n}\right)\triangleq(n,\ldots,n,j,\ldots,j)
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
is defined in Appendix
E.1
.
In view of the above notation, we now consider any arbitrary
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and
\dagger\in\{0,n\}
. We first observe that

\displaystyle v_{j,\dagger}=\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{j}^{\prime}=\dagger}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}=\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}^{*}:i_{j}^{\prime}=\dagger}\lambda_{{i}_{1}^{\prime}\cdots{i}_{n}^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{n-1}\lambda_{\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{1}^{j^{\prime}}\cdots\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{n}^{j^{\prime}}},&\text{if }\dagger=0,\\
\lambda_{n\cdots n}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j}^{n-1}\lambda_{\bar{i}_{1}^{j^{\prime}}\cdots\bar{i}_{n}^{j^{\prime}}},&\text{if }\dagger=n,\end{cases}

(31)

where the first equality follows from the fact that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
), the second equality holds because
\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}>0
only if
(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}^{*}\cup\{(n,\ldots,n)\}
, and the third equality holds because a tuple
(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}^{*}\cup\{(n,\ldots,n)\}
satisfies the equality
i_{j}^{\prime}=\dagger
if and only if
[(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})=(n,\ldots,n)\text{ and }\dagger=n]
or
[\dagger=0\textnormal{ and there exists $j^{\prime}\in\{j,\ldots,n\}$ such that }(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})=(\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{1}^{j^{\prime}},\ldots,\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{n}^{j^{\prime}})]
or
[\dagger=n\textnormal{ and there exists $j^{\prime}\in\{j,\ldots,n\}$ such that }(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})=(\bar{i}_{1}^{j^{\prime}},\ldots,\bar{i}_{n}^{j^{\prime}})]
. Using identical reasoning as above, we also observe that

\displaystyle v_{j+1,\dagger}=\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{j+1}^{\prime}=\dagger}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}=\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}^{*}:i_{j+1}^{\prime}=\dagger}\lambda_{{i}_{1}^{\prime}\cdots{i}_{n}^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\sum_{j^{\prime}=j+1}^{n-1}\lambda_{\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{1}^{j^{\prime}}\cdots\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{n}^{j^{\prime}}},&\text{if }\dagger=0,\\
\lambda_{n\cdots n}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=j+1}^{n-1}\lambda_{\bar{i}_{1}^{j^{\prime}}\cdots\bar{i}_{n}^{j^{\prime}}},&\text{if }\dagger=n.\end{cases}

(32)

Combining lines (
31
) and (
32
), we have shown that

\displaystyle v_{j,\dagger}-v_{j+1,\dagger}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{1}^{j}\cdots\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{n}^{j}},&\text{if }\dagger=0,\\
\lambda_{\bar{i}_{1}^{j}\cdots\bar{i}_{n}^{j}},&\text{if }\dagger=n.\end{cases}

(33)

We also recall from (
24
) that

\displaystyle v_{j,\dagger}-v_{j+1,\dagger}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n},&\text{if }\dagger=0\text{ and }j\in\bar{S},\\
\frac{1}{n},&\text{if }\dagger=n\text{ and }j\notin\bar{S},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(34)

Therefore, combining lines (
33
) and (
34
), we have shown that the following equalities hold for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{1}^{j}\cdots\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{n}^{j}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n},&\text{if }j\in\bar{S},\\
0,&\text{otherwise};\end{cases}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\bar{i}_{1}^{j}\cdots\bar{i}_{n}^{j}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n},&\text{if }j\notin\bar{S},\\
0,&\text{otherwise};\end{cases}

(35)

Moreover, it follows from the fact that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
) that

\displaystyle\lambda_{n\cdots n}

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}:(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\neq(n,\ldots,n)}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}^{*}}\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}}\lambda_{\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{1}^{j^{\prime}}\cdots\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{n}^{j^{\prime}}}-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}}\lambda_{\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{1}^{j^{\prime}}\cdots\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}_{n}^{j^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}}\frac{1}{n}-\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}}\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{n-1}{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n},

where the first equality follows from the fact that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
), the second equality follows from the fact that
\lambda_{i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots i_{n}^{\prime}}>0
for a tuple
(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}\setminus\{(n,\ldots,n)\}
if and only if
(i_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,i_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{L}^{*}
, the third and fourth equalities follow from (
35
), and the fourth and fifth equalities follow from algebra.
Combining the above analysis, we conclude that any feasible solution
\lambda
of the system (
23
) must satisfy the following equality must hold for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n},&\textnormal{if there exists }j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}\textnormal{ such that }(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})=\left(\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{n}\right),\\
\frac{1}{n},&\textnormal{if there exists }j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}\textnormal{ such that }(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})=\left(\bar{i}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}^{j}_{n}\right),\\
\frac{1}{n},&\textnormal{if }(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})=(n,\ldots,n),\\
0,&\textnormal{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(
25
)

We conclude the proof of Lemma
E.1
by showing, for the sake of completeness, that if
\lambda
satisfies the equality on line (
25
) for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
, then
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
). Indeed, let
\lambda
satisfy the equality on line (
25
) for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
. For each assortment
m\in\mathcal{M}
and product
i\in S_{m}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{m}=i}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n},&\text{if }i\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S},\\
\frac{1}{n},&\text{if }i\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\},\\
\frac{1}{n}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}:j\geq m}\frac{1}{n},&\text{if }i=n,\\
\sum_{j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}:j\geq m}\frac{1}{n},&\text{if }i=0\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n},&\text{if }i\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\{0,n\},\\
\frac{1}{n}\left(1+\left|\left\{m,\ldots,n-1\right\}\setminus\bar{S}\right|\right),&\text{if }i=n,\\
\frac{1}{n}\left|\left\{m,\ldots,n-1\right\}\cap\bar{S}\right|,&\text{if }i=0,\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle=v_{m,i},

where the first equality follows from the fact that
\lambda
satisfies the equality on line (
25
) for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
, the second equality follows from algebra, and the third equality follows from line (
24
). Furthermore, we observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}}\frac{1}{n}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}}\frac{1}{n}+\frac{1}{n}=\frac{|\mathcal{N}\setminus\{0,n\}|}{n}+\frac{1}{n}=1,

where the first equality follows from the fact that
\lambda
satisfies the equality on line (
25
) for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
, and the second and third equalities follows from algebra. Finally, we observe from line (
25
) that
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}\geq 0
for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}
. We have thus proven that
\lambda
is a feasible solution of the system (
23
), which concludes our proof of Lemma
E.1
.

\halmos

E.3
Proof of Theorem
4.9

We now use the above Lemma
E.1
to conclude our proof of Theorem
4.9
. We begin by using Lemma
E.1
to reformulate the robust optimization problem (
RO
). Indeed,

(
RO
)

\displaystyle=\max_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{\lambda}{\textnormal{minimize}}&&\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{m}=i}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}=v_{m,i}&&\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}\\
&&&\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}=1\\
&&&\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}\geq 0&&\forall(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{L},\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle=\max_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}}\rho_{\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{n}}(S)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}}\rho_{\bar{i}^{j}_{1},\ldots,\bar{i}^{j}_{n}}(S)+\frac{1}{n}\rho_{n\cdots n}(S)\right\},

(36)

where the first equality follows from Proposition
4.13
and the fact that
\eta=0
, and the second equality follows from Lemma
E.1
.

We next analyze the objective function in (
36
). Indeed, it follows readily from Proposition
B.11
from Appendix
B.2
that the following equalities hold for all assortments
S\in\mathcal{S}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \rho_{\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{1}\cdots\text{\@text@baccent{$i$}}^{j}_{n}}(S)&=r_{j}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\in S\right\}&&\forall j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\},\\
\rho_{\bar{i}^{j}_{1}\cdots\bar{i}^{j}_{n}}(S)&=r_{j}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\in S\right\}+r_{n}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\notin S\textnormal{ and }n\in S\right\}&&\forall j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S},\\
\rho_{n\cdots n}(S)&=r_{n}\mathbb{I}\left\{n\in S\right\}.\end{aligned}

Using the above equalities, we rewrite the optimization problem on line (
36
) as

\displaystyle\max_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\left\{\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}}r_{j}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\in S\right\}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}}\left(r_{j}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\in S\right\}+r_{n}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\notin S\textnormal{ and }n\in S\right\}\right)+\frac{1}{n}r_{n}\mathbb{I}\left\{n\in S\right\}}_{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}(S)}\right\}.

(37)

We next show that every optimal solution
S
of (
37
) must contain the product
n
. Indeed, for every arbitrary assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
that satisfies
n\notin S
, we observe from algebra that

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}^{\prime}(S\cup\{n\})-\mathscr{R}^{\prime}(S)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}}r_{n}\left(\mathbb{I}\left\{j\notin S\right\}-0\right)+\frac{1}{n}r_{n}>0.

Since the assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
with
n\notin S
was chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that every optimal solution
S
of (
37
) must satisfy
n\in S
. We can thus rewrite (
37
) as

\displaystyle\max_{S\in\mathcal{S}:n\in S}\left\{\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}}r_{j}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\in S\right\}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}}\left(r_{j}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\in S\right\}+r_{n}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\notin S\right\}\right)+\frac{1}{n}r_{n}}_{\mathscr{R}^{\prime\prime}(S)}\right\}.

(38)

We next show that every optimal solution
S
of the optimization problem (
38
) must satisfy
j^{\prime}\notin S
for all
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}
. Indeed, for every arbitrary assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
that satisfies
n\in S
and satisfies
j^{\prime}\in S
for some product
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}
, we observe from algebra that

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}^{\prime\prime}(S\setminus\{j^{\prime}\})-\mathscr{R}^{\prime\prime}(S)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(0+r_{n}\right)-\frac{1}{n}\left(r_{j^{\prime}}+0\right)>0.

Since the assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
with
n\in S
and
j^{\prime}\in S
for some
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}
was chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that every optimal solution
S
of (
38
) must satisfy
j^{\prime}\notin S
for all
j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}
. We can thus rewrite (
38
) as

\displaystyle\max_{S\in\mathcal{S}:n\in S,\;j^{\prime}\notin S\forall j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}}\left\{\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in\bar{S}\setminus\{0,n\}}r_{j}\mathbb{I}\left\{j\in S\right\}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}\setminus\bar{S}}r_{n}+\frac{1}{n}r_{n}}_{\mathscr{R}^{\prime\prime}(S)}\right\}.

(39)

Finally, we conclude by inspection that every optimal solution
S
of the optimization problem (
39
) must satsify
j^{\prime}\in S
for all
j^{\prime}\in S\setminus\{0,n\}
. We have thus shown that the only optimal solution of the robust optimization problem (
RO
) is
\bar{S}
, which concludes our proof of Theorem
4.9
.
\halmos

Appendix F
Assumption that
\mathcal{N}_{0}=\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}

In Remark
5.1
of §
5
, we state an assumption that the universe of products
\mathcal{N}_{0}
is equal to
\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}
, where
S_{1},\ldots,S_{M}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
are the assortments that the firm has previously offered to its customers. In that remark, we claim that this assumption can always be satisfied by redefining
\mathcal{N}_{0}
to be equal to
\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}
. In this appendix, we argue that this assumption is without loss of generality from the perspective of the robust optimization problem (
RO
). Specifically, we formalize Remark
5.1
via the following proposition:

Proposition F.1

\max\limits_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\min\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)=\max\limits_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\min\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right).

The above proposition says that if
S^{*}\in\mathcal{S}
is an optimal solution of the robust optimization problem (
RO
), then
S^{*}\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)
is also an optimal solution of the robust optimization problem (
RO
). Hence, the above proposition implies that we can assume without loss of generality that the robust optimization problem (
RO
) will only optimize over assortments
S
in the set
\mathcal{S}\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)
. The following proof of Proposition
F.1
relies on the graphical interpretation of
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
that is developed in Appendix
B.2
.

Proof F.2

Proof of Proposition
F.1
.
We first observe that

\displaystyle\max\limits_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\min\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\max\limits_{S\in\mathcal{S}\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)}\min\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}\left(S\right)\leq\max\limits_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\min\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S),

where the equality follows from the fact that
\mathcal{S}\triangleq\{S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}:0\in S\}
, and the inequality follows from algebra. To show the other direction,
consider any arbitrary assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
. We recall from Proposition
4.13
that the worst-case expected revenue
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right)
is equal to the optimal objective value of the following linear optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{\lambda,\epsilon}{\textnormal{minimize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

(40)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\textnormal{same constraints as \eqref{prob:robust_simplified}}

Moreover, we recall from Proposition
B.11
in Appendix
B.2
that the following equality holds for each
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
:

\displaystyle\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right)=\min_{i\in S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right)}r_{i}.

(41)

In particular, we observe for each
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\;\textnormal{for all }m\in\mathcal{M},\;\textnormal{if }i_{m}\in S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right),\text{ then }i_{m}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\;\textnormal{for all }m\in\mathcal{M},\;\textnormal{if }i_{m}\in S,\text{ then }i_{m}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\right),

(42)

where the first equality follows from Definition
B.10
, the second equality follows from the fact that
i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}\in\cup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}
, and the third equality follows from Definition
B.10
. Therefore, for every feasible solution
(\lambda,\epsilon)
for the linear optimization problem (
2
), we observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\left(\min_{i\in S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right)}r_{i}\right)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\left(\min_{i\in S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\right)}r_{i}\right)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\left(\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\right)}r_{i}\right)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\left(S\right)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}},

where the first equality follows from (
41
), the second equality follows from (
42
), the inequality follows from algebra, and the third equality follows from Proposition
B.11
. Since the above analysis holds for every feasible solution
(\lambda,\epsilon)
for the linear optimization problem (
2
), and since the assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that

\displaystyle\max\limits_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\min\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}\left(S\cap\left(\bigcup_{m\in\mathcal{M}}S_{m}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\max\limits_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\min\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S).

The proof of Proposition
F.1
is thus complete.
\halmos

Appendix G
Proofs of Technical Results from §
5.1

G.1
Proof of Lemma
5.3

It follows from Definition
4.11
and the fact that
M=2
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\left\{(i_{1},i_{2}):\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}(S_{1})\cap\mathcal{D}_{i_{2}}(S_{2})\neq\emptyset\right\}.

The rest of the proof of Lemma
5.3
is split into three cases.

In the first case, consider any arbitrary pair of products
(i_{1},i_{2})\in(S_{1}\setminus S_{2})\times S_{2}
. For this pair of products, consider any ranking
\sigma\in\Sigma
which satisfies the equalities
\sigma(i_{1})=0
and
\sigma(i_{2})=1
. It follows from the fact that
i_{1}\in S_{1}
that this ranking satisfies the equality
\argmin_{j\in S_{1}}\sigma(j)=1
. Moreover, it follows from the facts that
i_{1}\notin S_{2}
and
i_{2}\in S_{2}
that this ranking also satisfies the equality
\argmin_{j\in S_{2}}\sigma(j)=2
. We have thus shown that
\sigma\in\mathcal{D}_{1}(S_{1})\cap\mathcal{D}_{2}(S_{2})
, which proves that
\mathcal{D}_{1}(S_{1})\cap\mathcal{D}_{2}(S_{2})\neq\emptyset
. Since the pair of products
(i_{1},i_{2})\in(S_{1}\setminus S_{2})\times S_{2}
was chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that
(S_{1}\setminus S_{2})\times S_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
.

In the second case, consider any arbitrary pair of products
(i_{1},i_{2})\in S_{1}\times(S_{2}\setminus S_{1})
. Using identical reasoning as the first case, we observe that
\mathcal{D}_{1}(S_{1})\cap\mathcal{D}_{2}(S_{2})\neq\emptyset
, which shows that
S_{1}\times(S_{2}\setminus S_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{L}
.

In the third case, consider any arbitrary pair of products
(i_{1},i_{2})\in(S_{1}\cap S_{2})\times(S_{1}\cap S_{2})
. For this pair of products, it follows from Definition
4.10
that the inequality
\sigma(i_{1})<\sigma(j)
must hold for all rankings
\sigma\in\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}(S_{1})
and all products
j\in(S_{1}\cap S_{2})\setminus\{i_{1}\}
. It also follows from Definition
4.10
that the inequality
\sigma(i_{2})<\sigma(j)
must hold for all rankings
\sigma\in\mathcal{D}_{i_{2}}(S_{2})
and all products
j\in(S_{1}\cap S_{2})\setminus\{i_{2}\}
. It follows immediately from these inequalities that there exists a ranking that satisfies
\sigma\in\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}}(S_{1})\cap\mathcal{D}_{i_{2}}(S_{2})
if and only if
i_{1}=i_{2}
, and so it follows from Definition
4.11
that
(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}
if and only if
i_{1}=i_{2}
.

Since the three cases we have considered are exhaustive, our proof of Lemma
5.3
is complete.
\halmos

G.2
Proof of Lemma
5.4

Let
M=2
and
\eta=0
. In the remainder of the proof of Lemma
5.4
, we will show that the following equality holds for all assortments
S\in\mathcal{S}
:

\displaystyle\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}}\rho_{ii}(S)v_{1,i}\;+

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\left[\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{\lambda}{\textnormal{minimize}}&&\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}}\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}\\
&&&+\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}}\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}\\
&&&+\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}}\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}}(\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)-\rho_{i_{1}i_{1}}(S))\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{2}}\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}=v_{1,i_{1}}&&\forall i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}\\
&\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}}\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}=v_{2,i_{2}}&&\forall i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}\\
&\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}}\lambda_{ii_{2}}-\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}\lambda_{i_{1}i}=v_{1,i}-v_{2,i}&&\forall i\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}\\
&\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}\lambda_{i_{1}i}\leq v_{2,i}&&\forall i\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}\\
&\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}\geq 0&&\forall(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\right].

(43)

We readily observe that the linear optimization problem on line (
43
) is a minimum-cost network flow problem, where each decision variable
\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}
corresponds to the flow on a directed edge from vertex
i_{1}
to vertex
i_{2}

(
Ahuja et al. 1988
, p. 296)
. In particular, we observe that the minimum-cost network flow problem on line (
43
) takes place on a tripartite directed acyclic graph with
|S_{1}\setminus S_{2}|+|S_{2}\setminus S_{1}|+|S_{1}\cap S_{2}|=n+1
vertices and
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
directed edges. In Figure
G.2
, we present a visualization of the network corresponding to the minimum-cost network flow problem from line (
43
).

Remark G.1

The inequality of the form
\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}\lambda_{i_{1}i}\leq v_{2,i}
for each vertex
i\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}
can be interpreted as an upper bound on the incoming flow entering into vertex
i
. These inequalities can be converted into edge capacities by splitting each vertex
i\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}
into two vertices,
i^{\text{in}}
and
i^{\text{out}}
, where the incoming edges to vertex
i^{\text{in}}
are from the vertices in
S_{1}\setminus S_{2}
, the outgoing edges from
i^{\text{out}}
are to the vertices in
S_{2}\setminus S_{1}
, the only outgoing edge from vertex
i^{\text{in}}
is the only incoming edge to vertex
i^{\text{out}}
, the flow through the edge from vertex
i^{\text{in}}
to vertex
i^{\text{out}}
must satisfy the constraints
0\leq g_{i^{\text{in}}i^{\text{out}}}\leq v_{2,i}
, and the supplies on the vertices
i^{\text{in}}
and
i^{\text{out}}
are
0
and
v_{1,i}-v_{2,i}
, respectively.

\FIGURE
1
0
5
4
3
2

Visualization of minimum-cost network flow problem from line (
43
).

\TABLEfootnotesizeIX
The figure shows a visualization of the minimum-cost network flow problem corresponding to the linear optimization problem on line (
43
) for the case where the past assortments are
S_{1}=\{0,1,2,5\}
and
S_{2}=\{0,3,4,5\}
. We see that there is a vertex in the graph for each product
i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}\equiv\{0,1,2,3,4,5\}
. The graph is a tripartite directed graph, where the three partitions of vertices are denoted by the dotted ellipses and correspond to
S_{1}\setminus S_{2}=\{1,2\}
,
S_{1}\cap S_{2}=\{0,5\}
, and
S_{2}\setminus S_{1}=\{3,4\}
. The flow demands at each of the vertices and the flow cost for each of the directed edges can be found on line (
43
).

We now proceed to prove that line (
43
) holds. Indeed, consider any arbitrary assortment
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
. It follows from Proposition
4.13
and our assumptions of
M=2
and
\eta=0
that

\displaystyle\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)=\left[\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{\lambda}{\textnormal{minimize}}&&\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{1}=i}\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}=v_{1,i}\quad\forall i\in S_{1}\\
&&&\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{2}=i}\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}=v_{2,i}\quad\forall i\in S_{2}\\
&&&\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}}\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}=1\\
&&&\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}\geq 0\quad\forall(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}\end{aligned}\right].

After applying Lemma
5.3
to the above optimization problem and removing the redundant constraint
\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}}\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}=1
, we observe that

\displaystyle\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)=\left[\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{\lambda}{\textnormal{minimize}}&&\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}}\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}+\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}}\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}\\
&&&+\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}}\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}}\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}+\sum_{i\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}}\rho_{ii}(S)\lambda_{ii}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\begin{aligned} &\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{2}}\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}=v_{1,i_{1}}&&\forall i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}\\
&\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}}\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}=v_{2,i_{2}}&&\forall i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}\\
&\lambda_{ii}+\sum_{i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}}\lambda_{ii_{2}}=v_{1,i}&&\forall i\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}\\
&\lambda_{ii}+\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}\lambda_{i_{1}i}=v_{2,i}&&\forall i\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}\\
&\lambda_{i_{1}i_{2}}\geq 0&&\forall(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}\end{aligned}\end{aligned}\right].

Finally, for each
i\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}
, we eliminate the decision variable
\lambda_{ii}
from the above optimization problem by using the equality
\lambda_{ii}=v_{2,i}-\sum_{i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}\lambda_{i_{1}i}
. With this elimination, we obtain the desired result.
\halmos

G.3
Proof of Theorem
5.2

We first describe our algorithm for solving the robust optimization problem (
RO
), which follows a brute-force strategy. Namely, our algorithm iterates over each of the assortments
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, and, for each such assortment, the algorithm computes the corresponding worst-case expected revenue
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
. The algorithm concludes by returning the maximum value of
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
across the assortments
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
. The correctness of this algorithm for solving the robust optimization problem (
RO
) follows immediately from Theorem
4.1
.

We now analyze the running time of our algorithm by using our three intermediary results.
We assume that the two assortments
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are given as sorted arrays. Under this assumption,
it is straightforward to see that the sets
S_{1}\cap S_{2}
,
S_{1}\setminus S_{2}
, and
S_{2}\setminus S_{1}
can be computed and stored as sorted arrays in
\mathcal{O}(n)
computation time. We also require
\mathcal{O}(n)
computation time to store copies of the sets
S_{1},S_{2},S_{1}\cap S_{2}
,
S_{1}\setminus S_{2}
, and
S_{2}\setminus S_{1}
in hash tables, which ensures that querying whether a given product is an element of any of these sets can be performed in
\mathcal{O}(1)
time.

We next analyze the computation times for constructing the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, constructing the set of pairs of products
\mathcal{L}
, and computing the quantities
\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)
for each assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and each pair of products
(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}
. Indeed, using the aforementioned data structures, it follows readily from
Lemma
4.4
that we can construct the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
in
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
computation time.
19
19

19

It follows from Lemma
4.4
that we can efficiently iterate over the assortments in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
by iterating over the pairs of products in
S_{1}\setminus S_{2}
and
S_{2}\setminus S_{1}
. Constructing the collection
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
thus requires iterating over the
|S_{1}\setminus S_{2}|\times|S_{2}\setminus S_{1}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
assortments, and each of the assortments is comprised of at most
\mathcal{O}(n)
products.

Moreover, it follows from
Lemma
5.3
that the set of pairs of products
\mathcal{L}
can be computed in
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
time. Finally, we analyze the computation times for computing the quantities
\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)
for each assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and each pair of products
(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}
. Indeed, we recall from Lemma
4.4
that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
, and we recall from Lemma
5.3
that
|\mathcal{L}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
. Therefore, there are
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|\times|\mathcal{L}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{4})
different ways of choosing an assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and a pair of products
(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}
. For each assortment
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
and pair of products
(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}
, it follows readily from Definition
4.12
and Lemmas
4.4
and
5.3
that

\displaystyle\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}r_{i_{1}},&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1},i_{2}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}},\;i_{1}=i_{2},\textnormal{ and }i_{1}\in S,\\
0,&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1},i_{2}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}},\;i_{1}=i_{2},\textnormal{ and }i_{1}\notin S,\\
\\[-5.0pt]
r_{i_{2}},&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}},\textnormal{ and }i_{2}\in S,\\
\min\left\{r_{i_{1}},\min_{j\in S\cap S_{2}\setminus S_{1}}r_{j}\right\},&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}},\;i_{2}\notin S,\textnormal{ and }i_{1}\in S,\\
0,&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}},\;i_{2}\notin S,\textnormal{ and }i_{1}\notin S,\\
\\[-5.0pt]
r_{i_{1}},&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}},\textnormal{ and }i_{1}\in S,\\
\min\left\{r_{i_{2}},\min_{j\in S\cap S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}r_{j}\right\},&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}},\;i_{1}\notin S,\textnormal{ and }i_{2}\in S,\\
0,&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{1}\cap S_{2}},\;i_{1}\notin S,\textnormal{ and }i_{2}\notin S,\\
\\[-5.0pt]
\min\left\{r_{i_{1}},r_{i_{2}}\right\},&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}},\;i_{1}\in S,\text{ and }i_{2}\in S,\\
\min\left\{r_{i_{1}},\min_{j\in S\cap S_{2}\setminus S_{1}}r_{j}\right\},&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}},\;i_{1}\in S,\text{ and }i_{2}\notin S,\\
\min\left\{r_{i_{2}},\min_{j\in S\cap S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}r_{j}\right\},&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}},\;i_{1}\notin S,\text{ and }i_{2}\in S,\\
0,&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i_{1}\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2},\;i_{2}\in S_{2}\setminus S_{1}},\;i_{1}\notin S,\text{ and }i_{2}\notin S.\end{cases}

We observe that the quantities
\min_{j\in S\cap S_{1}\setminus S_{2}}r_{j}
and
\min_{j\in S\cap S_{2}\setminus S_{1}}r_{j}
appear in many of the above cases, and we see that these quantities can be precomputed for each of the assortments
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
in a total of
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|\times\mathcal{O}(n)=\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
computation time. Given that we have precomputed these quantities, and given the fact that our data structures allow us to query whether any product is an element of the sets
S_{1}\setminus S_{2}
,
S_{2}\setminus S_{1}
, and
S_{1}\cap S_{2}
in
\mathcal{O}(1)
time, we conclude that all of the
\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)
can be computed in a total of
\mathcal{O}(n^{4}+n^{3})=\mathcal{O}(n^{4})
time.
In summary, we have established that constructing the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, constructing the set of pairs of products
\mathcal{L}
, and computing the quantities
\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)
for each assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and each pair of products
(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}
can be performed in a total of
\mathcal{O}(n^{4})
computation time.

We conclude our proof of Theorem
5.2
by establishing the total computation time of our brute-force algorithm using the information computed above. In each iteration of our algorithm, we select an assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and compute the worst-case expected revenue
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
. As shown in Lemma
5.4
, we can compute
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
by solving a minimum-cost network flow problem over a graph with
n+1
vertices and
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
edges. Using the minimum-cost network flow algorithm of
Orlin 1997
and
Tarjan 1997
, we observe that Problem (
43
) can be solved in
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\log(nr_{n}))
computation time.
20
20

20

The algorithm of
Orlin 1997
and
Tarjan 1997
computes the minimum-cost network flow on a directed graph in
\mathcal{O}((VE\log V)\min\left\{\log(VC),E\log V\right\})
running time, where
V
is the number of vertices,
E
is the number of directed edges, and
C
is the maximum absolute value of any edge cost. The algorithm requires that
C
is integral; for more details, see
Tarjan 1997
. In our case, Problem (
43
) is a minimum-cost network flow problem in a directed graph where
V=n
,
E=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
, and
C=\max_{S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}},(i_{1},i_{2})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}i_{2}}(S)=r_{n}
.
Since the worst-case expected revenue
