\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
must be computed for each assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, and since it follows readily from Lemma
4.4
that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
, we conclude that our algorithm requires a total of
\mathcal{O}(n^{5}\log(nr_{n}))
computation time.

\halmos

Appendix H
Proofs of Technical Results from §
5.2

H.1
Proof of Lemma
5.6

To construct the set of tuples of products
\mathcal{L}
, we iterate over each tuple of products
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{M}
. For each such tuple of products, we can construct the corresponding directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
that is described in the beginning of Appendix
B.2
. According to Lemma
B.1
in Appendix
B.2
, the tuple of products
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})
satisfies
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
if and only if the directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
is acyclic. We can check whether a directed graph is acyclic by using the well-known topological sorting algorithm
(
Ahuja et al. 1988
, p.79)
. Therefore, our algorithm for constructing the set of tuples of products
\mathcal{L}
is to iterate over each tuple of products
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{M}
and, for each such tuple of products, to check whether the tuple of products satisfies
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
by performing the topological sorting algorithm on the directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
.

The computation time of the above algorithm for constructing
\mathcal{L}
can be analyzed as follows. The number of iterations in the algorithm is equal to
|S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{M}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{M})
, where we observe that it is trivial from a computation time analysis to enumerate and iterate over the tuples of products in
S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{M}
. For each iteration, we must construct a directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
that has
n+1=\mathcal{O}(n)
vertices and
\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}}(|S_{m}|+1)=\mathcal{O}(Mn)
directed edges. Constructing this directed graph requires examining each product in each of the assortments
S_{1},\ldots,S_{M}
, and so we can construct
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
in
\mathcal{O}(Mn)
computation time. The computation time for the topological sort algorithm on a directed graph is equal to the number of vertices plus the number of directed edges in the directed graph, and so we can check whether
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
is acyclic in
\mathcal{O}(n+Mn)=\mathcal{O}(Mn)
computation time. Therefore, we have shown that our algorithm for constructing the set of tuples of products
\mathcal{L}
requires
\mathcal{O}\left(n^{M}\times\left(Mn+Mn\right)\right)=\mathcal{O}(Mn^{M+1})
computation time. We also observe from the inequality
|\mathcal{L}|\leq|S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{M}|
that
|\mathcal{L}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{M})
, which concludes our proof of Lemma
5.6
.

\halmos

H.2
Proof of Lemma
5.7

Consider any assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and any tuple of products
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
. To motivate our algorithm for computing
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
, we begin by recalling the directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
corresponding to the tuple of products
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})
that is described in the beginning of Appendix
B.2
. According to Lemma
B.1
in Appendix
B.2
, it follows from the fact that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
that
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
is a directed acyclic graph. Moreover, we recall that

\displaystyle\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)}r_{i}=\min_{i\in S:\;i_{m}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i\textnormal{ for all }i_{m}\in S}r_{i},

(44)

where the first equality follows from Proposition
B.11
in Appendix
B.2
and the second equality follows from Definition
B.10
in Appendix
B.2
. We recall from Definition
B.3
in Appendix
B.2
that the notation
i\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j
means that there is no directed path from vertex
j
to vertex
i
in the graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
. Stated in words, line (
44
) shows that
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
is equal to the minimum revenue
r_{i}
among all of the vertices
i
in the directed acyclic graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
which do not have a directed path to a vertex
i_{m}
for any past assortment
m\in\mathcal{M}
which satisfies
i_{m}\in S
.

Based on the above observations, we arrive at the following straightforward algorithm (presented as Algorithm
4
) for computing
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
. The algorithm begins on line (
1
) of Algorithm
4
by constructing the directed acyclic graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
. In particular, it follows from identical reasoning as in the proof of Lemma
5.6
that
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
can be constructed in
\mathcal{O}(Mn)
computation time and that this graph is comprised of
\mathcal{O}(n)
vertices and
\mathcal{O}(Mn)
directed edges. In line (
2
) of Algorithm
4
, we iterate over each of the previously offered assortments
m\in\mathcal{M}
which satisfy
i_{m}\in S
. For each such past assortment
m
, we mark all of the vertices in the graph which have a directed path to vertex
i_{m}
. Since the graph has
\mathcal{O}(Mn)
directed edges, and assuming henceforth that
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
is stored in memory as an adjacency list, it is easy to see using a standard graph traversal algorithm like depth-first search that the loop in line (
2
) of Algorithm
4
can be performed in a total of
\mathcal{O}(M\times Mn)=\mathcal{O}(M^{2}n)
computation time. Finally, line (
3
) of Algorithm
4
iterates over all of the vertices in at most
\mathcal{O}(Mn)
computation time and outputs the minimum
r_{i}
among all of the vertices
i
which are unmarked. The correctness of Algorithm
4
follows immediately from our earlier reasoning on line (
44
), and the total computation time required for Algorithm
4
is

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathcal{O}\left(Mn\right)}_{\eqref{step:construct_rho:1}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}n\right)}_{\eqref{step:construct_rho:2}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{O}\left(n\right)}_{\eqref{step:construct_rho:3}}=\mathcal{O}(M^{2}n).

This concludes our proof of Lemma
5.7
.
\halmos

Construct-
{{\rho}}(S,(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}),\mathscr{M},r)

Inputs
:

•

An assortment,
S\in\mathcal{S}
.

•

A tuple of products,
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
.

•

The collection of past assortments,
\mathscr{M}\equiv\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{M}\}
.

•

The revenues of the products,
r\equiv(r_{0},r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
.

Output
:

•

The quantity
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
.

Procedure
:

1.

Construct the directed acyclic graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
.

2.

For each past assortment
m\in\mathcal{M}
:

(a)

If
i_{m}\in S
:

i.

Mark each unmarked vertex in
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
that has a directed path to vertex
i_{m}
.

3.

Output the minimum
r_{i}
among all unmarked vertices
i
in
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
and terminate.

Algorithm 4:
A procedure for computing
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
.

H.3
Proof of Theorem
5.5

As described at the beginning of §
5.2
, we consider a brute-force algorithm for solving the robust optimization problem (
RO
) that consists of computing the worst-case expected revenue
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
for each candidate assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and outputting the assortment that has the maximum worst-case expected revenue. The computation time of this brute-force algorithm is analyzed as follows. In Lemmas
4.6
and
4.5
, we established that the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
can be constructed in
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(M+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|))=\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(M+(n+2)^{2^{M}}))=\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n))
computation time, where the last equality holds for any fixed
M
. In Lemma
5.6
, we established that the set of tuples of products
\mathcal{L}
can be constructed in
\mathcal{O}(Mn^{M+1})=\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n))
computation time, where the equality holds for any fixed
M
. Our algorithm performs an iteration for each assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, and thus our algorithm will perform
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|\leq(n+2)^{2^{M}}=\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n))
iterations for any fixed
M
. Given any assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, we established in Lemma
5.7
that the quantities
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
for each
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
can all be computed in a total of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{L}|\times M^{2}n)=\mathcal{O}(n^{M}\times M^{2}n)=\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n))
computation time, where the last equality holds for any fixed
M
. Given the set of tuples of products
\mathcal{L}
and the quantities
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
for each
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
, we established in Remark
4.15
that we can compute the worst-case expected revenue
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
by constructing and solving a linear optimization problem with
\mathcal{O}(n^{M})=\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n))
decision variables and
\mathcal{O}(nM)=\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n))
constraints for any fixed
M
. Since linear optimization can be solved in weakly polynomial-time via the ellipsoid algorithm, we conclude that each iteration of our algorithm requires
\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n))
time for any fixed
M
. Our proof of Theorem
5.5
is thus complete.
\halmos

Appendix I
Proofs of Technical Results from §
5.3

This appendix contains the proofs of the technical results from §
5.3
. Appendices
I.1
,
I.2
, and
I.3
contain the proofs of Lemma
5.8
, Corollary
5.9
, and Lemma
5.10
. Appendix
I.4
contains two intermediary results (Lemmas
I.2
and
I.5
) that are used in the proofs of Proposition
5.11
and Theorem
5.12
. Appendices
I.5
and
I.6
contain the proofs of Proposition
5.11
and Theorem
5.12
.
We remark that the proofs in this appendix make extensive use of the notation and intermediary results from §
4.4
and Appendix
B.2
.

I.1
Proof of Lemma
5.8

Let Assumption
5.3
hold, consider any tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{M}
, and consider the corresponding directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
. We recall that the set of vertices in the directed graph is equal to
\mathcal{N}_{0}
, and the graph has a directed edge
(i,i_{m})
from each vertex
i\in S_{m}\setminus\{i_{m}\}
to vertex
i_{m}
for each past assortment
m\in\mathcal{M}
.

To show the first direction, suppose that there exists an
m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
such that
i_{m+1}\notin\{i_{m}\}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m+1}
. In this case, we observe that

\displaystyle i_{m+1}\in S_{m+1}\setminus\left(\{i_{m}\}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m+1}\right)=S_{m+1}\setminus\left(\{i_{m}\}\cup\left(S_{m+1}\setminus S_{m}\right)\right)=S_{m}\setminus\{i_{m}\},

where the inclusion follows from the fact that
i_{m+1}\in S_{m+1}
and from the supposition that
i_{m+1}\notin\{i_{m}\}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m+1}
, the first equality follows from line (
3
) and from the fact that
m+1\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
, and the second equality follows from algebra.
Moreover, it follows from the fact that the past assortments are nested that
i_{m}\in S_{m+1}
and from the supposition that
i_{m+1}\neq i_{m}
that
(i_{m},i_{m+1})
is a directed edge in
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
.
Since the directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
has a directed edge from each vertex
i\in S_{m}\setminus\{i_{m}\}
to vertex
i_{m}
, we conclude that the directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
must have an edge from vertex
i_{m+1}
to vertex
i_{m}
, which implies that
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
has a cycle. Therefore, it follows from Lemma
B.1
from Appendix
B.2
that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\notin\mathcal{L}
.

To show the other direction, suppose that the inclusion
i_{m+1}\in\{i_{m}\}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m+1}
holds for all
m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
. For notational convenience, let
i_{m_{1}},\ldots,i_{m_{K}}
denote the elements of the set
\cup_{m=1}^{M}\{i_{m}\}
, where we assume without loss of generality that the indices
m_{1},\ldots,m_{K}
satisfy
m_{1}<\cdots<m_{K}
, satisfy
m_{1}=1
, and satisfy
i_{m_{k}-1}\neq i_{m_{k}}
for all
k\in\{2,\ldots,K\}
. It follows from the construction of the directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
that
i_{m_{1}},\ldots,i_{m_{K}}
are the only vertices in the directed graph with incoming edges. Moreover,
it follows from our choice of the indices
m_{1},\ldots,m_{K}
and from the supposition that
i_{m+1}\in\{i_{m}\}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m+1}
for all
m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
that
i_{m_{k}}\in\mathcal{B}_{m_{k}}
for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, which implies that
i_{m_{k^{\prime}}}\notin S_{m_{k}}
for all
1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq K
. Therefore, we conclude for all
1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq K
that there is no directed edge in
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
from vertex
i_{m_{k^{\prime}}}
to vertex
i_{m_{k}}
, which implies that
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
is acyclic. Therefore, it follows from Lemma
B.1
that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
.

\halmos

I.2
Proof of Corollary
5.9

Let Assumption
5.3
hold. For each
m\in\mathcal{M}
, let us define the following set of tuples:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{m}\triangleq\begin{cases}\left\{(i_{1}):i_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\right\},&\text{if }m=1,\\
\left\{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}):(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m-1})\in\mathcal{L}_{m-1}\textnormal{ and }i_{m}\in\{i_{m-1}\}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\},&\text{if }m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}.\end{cases}

(45)

It follows from a straightforward induction argument and from Lemma
5.8
that
\mathcal{L}_{M}=\mathcal{L}
. Moreover, it follows from a straightforward induction argument and from line (
45
) that the following holds for each
m\in\mathcal{M}
:

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{L}_{m}\right|=\begin{cases}\left|\mathcal{B}_{1}\right|,&\text{if }m=1,\\
\left|\mathcal{B}_{1}\right|\times\prod_{m^{\prime}=2}^{m}\left(1+\left|\mathcal{B}_{m^{\prime}}\right|\right),&\text{if }m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}.\end{cases}

(46)

Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{L}\right|=\left|\mathcal{B}_{1}\right|\times\prod_{m=2}^{M}\left(1+\left|\mathcal{B}_{m}\right|\right)\geq 2^{M-1},

where the equality follows from line (
46
) and from the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{M}=\mathcal{L}
, and the inequality follows from the fact that
|\mathcal{B}_{m}|\geq 1
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
. This completes our proof of Corollary
5.9
.

\halmos

I.3
Proof of Lemma
5.10

We assume throughout the proof of Lemma
5.10
that Assumption
5.3
holds, and that
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
is an assortment that satisfies
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
.

We begin by considering any tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
that satisfy
i_{m}\in S
. In this case, we recall from Appendix
B.2
that the graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
has a directed edge from each vertex
i\in S_{m}\setminus\{i_{m}\}
to vertex
i_{m}
. In particular, that implies that there is a directed edge from each vertex
i\in(S\cap S_{m})\setminus\{i_{m}\}
to vertex
i_{m}
. Since
i_{m}\in S
, we have thus shown that
i_{m}\prec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i
for all
i\in(S\cap S_{m})\setminus\{i_{m}\}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m}\cap\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j\right\}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m}:i=i_{m}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=r_{i_{m}},

(47)

where the first equality follows from Proposition
B.11
from Appendix
B.2
, the second equality follows from Definition
B.10
from Appendix
B.2
, the third equality follows from algebra, the fourth equality follows from the fact that
i_{m}\prec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i
for all
i\in(S\cap S_{m})\setminus\{i_{m}\}
, and the fifth equality follows from algebra. In summary, we have shown that if a tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
satisfy
i_{m}\in S
, then
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=r_{i_{m}}
.

We next consider any tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
that satisfies
i_{1}\notin S
.
For this case, we observe that

\displaystyle\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{1})

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{1})}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{1}\cap\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{1},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j\right\}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{1}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{1},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{1}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\{2,\ldots,M\},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{1},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{1}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}}r_{i}.

Indeed, the first equality follows from Proposition
B.11
. The second equality follows from Definition
B.10
. The third equality follows from algebra. The fourth equality follows from the fact that
i_{1}\notin S
. The fifth equality follows from Lemma
5.8
, which together with the facts that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
and
i_{1}\notin S
readily implies that
i_{2},\ldots,i_{M}\notin S\cap S_{1}
. The sixth equality follows from line (
3
). In summary, we have shown that if a tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
satisfies
i_{1}\notin S
, then
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{1})=\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{1}}r_{i}
.

Finally, we consider any tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
and
{m}\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
that satisfy
i_{m}\notin S
. In this case, we observe that

\displaystyle\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap(S_{m-1}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m})\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i},\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}\right\},

(48)

where the first equality follows from Proposition
B.11
, the second equality follows from Assumption
5.3
, and the third equality follows from algebra. In the remainder of the proof, we focus on evaluating the two terms
\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}
and
\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}
that appear in line (
48
). First, we observe that

\displaystyle\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m-1}\cap\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j\right\}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m-1}\cap\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j\right\}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m-1}\cap\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m-1},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j\right\}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m-1}\cap\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m-1},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j\right\}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1})}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1}).

(49)

Indeed, the first equality follows from Definition
B.10
. The second equality follows from Lemma
5.8
, which together with the facts that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
and
i_{m}\notin S
implies that
i_{m},\ldots,i_{M}\notin S\cap S_{m}
. The third equality follows from Assumption
5.3
, which together with the fact that
i_{m}\notin S
implies for each
m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
that
i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m}
if and only if
i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m-1}
. The fourth equality follows from the fact that
i_{m},\ldots,i_{M}\notin S\cap S_{m}
and the fact that
S\cap S_{m-1}\subseteq S\cap S_{m}
. The fifth equality follows from Definition
B.10
, and the sixth equality follows from Proposition
B.11
. Moreover, we observe that

\displaystyle\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j\right\}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j\right\}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap\left\{j\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m-1},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j\right\}}r_{i}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m-1},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i}r_{i}.

(50)

Indeed, the first equality follows from Definition
B.10
. The second equality follows from Lemma
5.8
, which together with the facts that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
and
i_{m}\notin S
implies that
i_{m},\ldots,i_{M}\notin S\cap S_{m}
. The third equality follows from Assumption
5.3
, which together with the fact that
i_{m}\notin S
implies for each
m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
that
i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m}
if and only if
i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m-1}
. To simplify the expression on line (
50
), we make use of the following intermediary claim.

Claim 10

Consider any
i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}
and
m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
. If
i_{m^{\prime}}\in S
, then
i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i
.

Proof I.1

Proof of Claim
10
.
Consider any
i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}
, and consider any
m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
that satisfies the inclusion
i_{m^{\prime}}\in S
.

We first show that
i_{m^{\prime\prime}}\prec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i_{m^{\prime}}
for all
m^{\prime\prime}\in\{m,\ldots,M\}
. Indeed, consider any arbitrary
m^{\prime\prime}\in\{m,\ldots,M\}
. We readily observe from Assumption
5.3
that
i_{m^{\prime}}\in S_{m^{\prime}}\subseteq S_{m^{\prime\prime}}
. Moreover, it follows from the fact that
i_{m^{\prime}}\in S
and from the fact that
i_{m}\notin S
that the expression
i_{m^{\prime}}\neq i_{m}
must hold. Combining the expression
i_{m^{\prime}}\neq i_{m}
with Lemma
5.8
, we observe that the expression
i_{m^{\prime}}\neq i_{m^{\prime\prime}}
must hold. Combining the expression
i_{m^{\prime}}\neq i_{m^{\prime\prime}}
with the fact that
i_{m^{\prime}},i_{m^{\prime\prime}}\in S_{m^{\prime\prime}}
, we conclude that the graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
must have a directed edge from vertex
i_{m^{\prime}}
to vertex
i_{m^{\prime\prime}}
. Because
m^{\prime\prime}\in\{m,\ldots,M\}
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
i_{m^{\prime\prime}}\prec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i_{m^{\prime}}
for all
m^{\prime\prime}\in\{m,\ldots,M\}
.

We next show that the vertex
i
in the graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
has an outgoing edge to vertex
j
only if there exists
m^{\prime\prime}\in\{m,\ldots,M\}
such that
j=i_{m^{\prime\prime}}
. Indeed, we observe from the fact that
i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{m}
and from Assumption
5.3
that the inclusion
i\in S_{m^{\prime\prime}}
holds if and only if
m^{\prime\prime}\in\{m,\ldots,M\}
. Therefore, it follows from the construction of the directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
that
(i,j)
is a directed edge in
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
only if
j=i_{m^{\prime\prime}}
for some
m^{\prime\prime}\in\{m,\ldots,M\}
.

We now combine the above steps to conclude the proof of Claim
10
. Indeed, suppose for the sake of developing a contradiction that
i_{m^{\prime}}\prec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i
. We showed previously that the only outgoing edges from the vertex
i
in the directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
are to vertices
j
that satisfy the equality
j=i_{m^{\prime\prime}}
for some
m^{\prime\prime}\in\{m,\ldots,M\}
. Therefore, it follows from the supposition that
i_{m^{\prime}}\prec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i
that there must exist a
m^{\prime\prime}\in\{m,\ldots,M\}
such that
i_{m^{\prime}}\prec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i_{m^{\prime\prime}}
. Because we showed previously that
i_{m^{\prime\prime}}\prec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i_{m^{\prime}}
, we conclude that the directed graph
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
must have a cycle. However, we recall from Lemma
B.1
from Appendix
B.2
and from the fact that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
that
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
is acyclic. Because we have a contradiction, we conclude that
i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i
, which completes our proof of Claim
10
.
\halmos

Combining lines (
48
), (
49
), and (
50
) with Claim
10
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\min_{i\in S\cap S_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i},\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1}),\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})}r_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1}),\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}:\textnormal{ for all }m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\},\textnormal{ if }i_{m^{\prime}}\in S\cap S_{m-1},\textnormal{ then }i_{m^{\prime}}\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}i}r_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1}),\min_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}}r_{i}\right\}.

Indeed, the first equality follows from (
48
), the second equality follows from (
49
), the third equality follows from (
50
), and the fourth equality follows from Claim
10
. Our proof of Lemma
5.10
is thus complete.

\halmos

I.4
Intermediary Results for Proposition
5.11
and Theorem
5.12

This appendix contains two intermediary lemmas that will be used in the proofs of Proposition
5.11
in Appendix
I.5
and the proof of Theorem
5.12
in Appendix
I.6
. The purpose of these intermediary lemmas, which are presented below as Lemmas
I.2
and
I.5
, is to characterize the relationship between the the linear optimization problem (
6
) and the linear optimization problem (
2
).

For notational convenience, let the following set be defined for each assortment
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}(S)\triangleq\left\{(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:\begin{gathered}\left[i\in S\textnormal{ and }i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}\right]\\
\textnormal{ or }\left[i\in S\textnormal{ and }i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}\right]\\
\textnormal{ or }\left[\kappa\in\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\setminus S\right\}\right]\end{gathered}\right\}.

We remark that
\mathfrak{N}(S)
is the set of vertices
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}
for which the linear optimization problem (
6
) has a constraint of the form
g_{m,i,\kappa}=0
. Moreover, let the set of all paths of the form
(1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M})\in\mathfrak{V}
in the directed acyclic graph
\mathfrak{G}\equiv(\mathfrak{V},\mathfrak{E})
be denoted by

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}\triangleq\left\{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M})):((m,i_{m},\kappa_{m}),(m+1,i_{m+1},\kappa_{m+1}))\in\mathfrak{E}\;\forall m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}\right\}.

In our first intermediary lemma, denoted below by Lemma
I.2
, we show that every feasible solution of the linear optimization problem (
2
) can be transformed into a feasible solution of the linear optimization problem (
6
) with the same objective value.

Lemma I.2

Let Assumption
5.3
hold, let
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
satisfy
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
, and let
(\lambda,\epsilon)
be a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
). For each vertex
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}
, let

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}},

and for each directed edge
((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{E}
, let

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa,i_{m+1}=i^{\prime},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m+1})=\kappa^{\prime}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}.

Then
(f,g,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
6
), and the objective value associated with
(f,g,\epsilon)
in the linear optimization problem (
6
) is equal to the objective value associated with
(\lambda,\epsilon)
in the linear optimization problem (
2
).

Proof I.3

Proof.
Let Assumption
5.3
hold, let
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
satisfy
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
, and let
(\lambda,\epsilon)
be a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
). For each vertex
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}
, let

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}},

and for each edge
((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{E}
, let

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa,i_{m+1}=i^{\prime},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m+1})=\kappa^{\prime}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}.

We begin by presenting an intermediary claim, denoted below by Claim
11
, which will provide the key ingredient in the proof of Lemma
I.2
. The purpose of this intermediary claim is to show that every tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
can be converted into a path
(1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M})
in which none of the vertices are elements of
\mathfrak{N}(S)
.

Claim 11

For each
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
, we have that
((1,i_{1},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{1})),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M})))\in\mathscr{P}
and that
(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m}))\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
.

Proof I.4

Proof of Claim
11
.
Consider any tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
. It follows readily from Definition
4.12
that the inclusion
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})\in\{r_{j}:j\in S\cap S_{m}\}
holds for each
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and so it follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{V}
that the inclusion
(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m}))\in\mathfrak{V}
holds for each
m\in\mathcal{M}
. Moreover, for every arbitrary
m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
, we observe from Lemma
5.8
that
i_{m+1}\in\{i_{m}\}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m+1}
, and we observe from Lemma
5.10
and from the fact that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
that
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m+1})\in\{\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})\}\cup\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\}
. Combining those two observations, we have shown that
((m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})),(m+1,i_{m+1},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m+1})))\in\mathfrak{E}
. Since
m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
((1,i_{1},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{1})),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M})))\in\mathscr{P}
.

To conclude the proof of Claim
11
, it remains for us to show that
(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m}))\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
. Indeed, for each
m\in\mathcal{M}
, we consider the following three cases:

•

If
i_{m}\in S
and
i_{m}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
, then it follows from Lemma
5.10
that
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=r_{i_{m}}
.

•

If
i_{m}\in S
and
i_{m}\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}
, then it follows from Lemma
5.10
that
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=r_{i_{m}}\notin\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\}
.

•

If
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})\in\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\}
, then it follows from Lemma
5.10
that
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})\in\{r_{j}:j\in S\cap\mathcal{B}_{m}\}
.

Combining the above cases with the definition of
\mathfrak{N}(S)
on line (
I.4
), we conclude that
(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m}))\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)
for each
m\in\mathcal{M}
. Our proof of Claim
11
is thus complete.

\halmos

Using Claim
11
, we show in the following bullet points that the solution
(f,g,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
6
); that is, we show that
(f,g,\epsilon)
satisfies each of the constraints in the linear optimization problem (
6
).

•

For all
m\in\mathcal{M}
and
i\in{S}_{m}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}g_{m,i,\kappa}-\epsilon_{m,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\left(\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)-\epsilon_{m,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i}\left(\sum_{\kappa:(m,i_{m},\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)-\epsilon_{m,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i}\mathbb{I}\left\{(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m}))\in\mathfrak{V}\right\}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}-\epsilon_{m,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}-\epsilon_{m,i}

\displaystyle=v_{m,i},

Indeed, the first equality follows from our construction of
g
. The second and third equalities follow from algebra. The fourth equality follows from Claim
11
, which implies that
(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m}))\in\mathfrak{V}
for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
. The fifth equality follows from the fact that
(\lambda,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
).

•

For all
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}
such that
m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{E}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{E}}\left(\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa,i_{m+1}=i^{\prime},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m+1})=\kappa^{\prime}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\left(\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{E},i_{m+1}=i^{\prime},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m+1})=\kappa^{\prime}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{((m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})),(m+1,i_{m+1},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m+1})))\in\mathfrak{E}\right\}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=g_{m,i,\kappa},

Indeed, the first equality follows from our construction of
f
. The second and third equalities follow from algebra. The fourth equality follows from Claim
11
, which implies that
((m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})),(m+1,i_{m+1},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m+1})))\in\mathfrak{E}
for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
. The fifth equality follows from our construction of
g
.

•

For all
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}
such that
m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in\mathfrak{E}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in\mathfrak{E}}\left(\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m-1}=i^{\prime},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1})=\kappa^{\prime},i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\left(\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})))\in\mathfrak{E},i_{m-1}=i^{\prime},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1})=\kappa^{\prime}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{((m-1,i_{m-1},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1})),(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})))\in\mathfrak{E}\right\}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=g_{m,i,\kappa},

Indeed, the first equality follows from our construction of
f
. The second and third equalities follow from algebra. The fourth equality follows from Claim
11
, which implies that
((m-1,i_{m-1},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1})),(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})))\in\mathfrak{E}
for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
. The fifth equality follows from our construction of
g
.

•

Consider any vertex
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}
that satisfies
g_{m,i,\kappa}>0
. Because
g_{m,i,\kappa}>0
, it follows from our construction of
g
that there must exist a tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
that satisfies
i_{m}=i
,
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})=\kappa
, and
\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}>0
. Since Claim
11
implies that
(m,i_{m},\rho_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m}))\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
and all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}=0\quad\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S).

•

We observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}g_{M,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\left(\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{M}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M})=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=1.

Indeed, the first equality follows from our construction of
g
. The second equality follows from Claim
11
, which implies that
(M,i_{M},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M}))\in\mathfrak{V}
for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
. The third equality follows from the fact that
(\lambda,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
).

Since
f,g
are clearly nonnegative, the above bullet points show that
(f,g,\epsilon)
satisfies each of the constraints in the linear optimization problem (
6
).

It remains for us to show that the objective value associated with
(f,g,\epsilon)
in the linear optimization problem (
6
) is equal to the objective value associated with
(\lambda,\epsilon)
in the linear optimization problem (
2
). Indeed, we observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa\left(\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{M}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M})=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa\left(\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{M}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)=\kappa}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\left(\sum_{i,\kappa:\;(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V},i_{M}=i,\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)=\kappa}\kappa\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\mathbb{I}\left\{(M,i_{M},\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S))\in\mathfrak{V}\right\}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}.

The first equality follows from the construction of
g
. The second equality follows from Remark
5.1
and Assumption
5.3
, which together imply that
S\cap S_{M}=S\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}=S
. The third and fourth equalities follows from algebra. The fifth equality follows from Claim
11
. Our proof of Lemma
I.2
is thus complete.
\halmos

Our second intermediary lemma, denoted below by Lemma
I.5
, will be used to show that the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
2
) is less than or equal to the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
6
).

Lemma I.5

Let Assumption
5.3
hold, let
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
satisfy
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
, and let
(f,g,\epsilon)
satisfy the following constraints:

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}g_{m,i,\kappa}-\epsilon_{m,i}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

\displaystyle\|\epsilon\|\leq\eta

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}g_{M,i,\kappa}=1

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}.

Then there exists a vector
\lambda
such that
(\lambda,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
6
) and

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\leq\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{m,i,\kappa}+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}r_{n}g_{m,i,\kappa}.

Proof I.6

Proof.
Let Assumption
5.3
hold, let
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
satisfy
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
, and let
(f,g,\epsilon)
satisfy the following constraints:

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}g_{m,i,\kappa}-\epsilon_{m,i}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

(54a)

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}

(54b)

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

(54c)

\displaystyle\|\epsilon\|\leq\eta

(54d)

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}g_{M,i,\kappa}=1

(54e)

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

(54f)

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}.

(54g)

We observe that
f
can be interpreted as a network flow through the directed acyclic graph
\mathfrak{G}\equiv(\mathfrak{V},\mathfrak{E})
, in the sense that
f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0
is the units of flow from vertex
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}
to vertex
(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{V}
, and the constraints of the form
\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{E}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}
and
\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in\mathfrak{E}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}
ensure that flow is conserved through each vertex.
Because
f
can be interpreted as a network flow in the directed acyclic graph
\mathfrak{G}\equiv(\mathfrak{V},\mathfrak{E})
, it follows from the flow decomposition theorem
(
Ahuja et al. 1988
, Theorem 3.5)
that there exists a nonnegative vector
\pi\equiv(\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}:((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P})\geq 0
that satisfies the following equalities:

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle=\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:i_{m}=i,\kappa_{m}=\kappa}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}

(55)

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:i_{m}=i,\kappa_{m}=\kappa,i_{m+1}=i^{\prime},\kappa_{m+1}=\kappa^{\prime}}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}

\displaystyle((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{E}.

From this point onward, consider any fixed choice of the nonnegative vector
\pi
that satisfies the above equalities. Given that choice of
\pi
, let the vector
\lambda\equiv(\pi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}:(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L})
be defined as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum_{\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{M}:((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\quad\forall(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}.

(56)

Our first main step of the proof of Lemma
I.5
consists of showing that
(\lambda,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
). To show that
(\lambda,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
), we will utilize the following property of
\mathscr{P}
:

Claim 12

If
((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}
, then
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
.

Proof I.7

Proof of Claim
12
.
Consider any path
((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}
. We readily observe from the construction of the graph
\mathfrak{G}\equiv(\mathfrak{V},\mathfrak{E})
and from the fact that
(1,i_{1},\kappa_{1})\in\mathfrak{V}
that
i_{1}\in S_{1}
. Next, choose any arbitrary
m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
. Because
(1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M})
is a path, we observe that there must exist a directed edge in the graph
\mathfrak{G}\equiv(\mathfrak{V},\mathfrak{E})
from vertex
(m-1,i_{m-1},\kappa_{m-1})\in\mathfrak{V}
to vertex
(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\in\mathfrak{V}
. Therefore, it follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{E}
that the inclusion
i_{m}\in\{i_{m-1}\}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m}
must hold. Because
m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
i_{1}\in S_{1}
and
i_{m}\in\{i_{m-1}\}\cup\mathcal{B}_{m}
for all
m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
, and so Lemma
5.8
implies that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
. Our proof of Claim
12
is thus complete.

\halmos

Equipped with Claim
12
, we now proceed to show that
(\lambda,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
). Indeed, for each
m\in\mathcal{M}
and
i\in S_{m}
, we observe that

\displaystyle v_{m,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}g_{m,i,\kappa}-\epsilon_{m,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:i_{m}=i,\kappa_{m}=\kappa}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)-\epsilon_{m,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:i_{m}=i}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}-\epsilon_{m,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:i_{m}=i}\left(\sum_{\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{M}:((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)-\epsilon_{m,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}-\epsilon_{m,i},

where the first equality follows from the fact that
(f,g,\epsilon)
satisfies the constraints (
54
), the second equality follows from line (
55
), the third equality follows from algebra, the fourth equality follows from algebra and Claim
12
, and the fifth equality follows from our construction of
\lambda
on line (
56
). Moreover, we observe that

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:i_{M}=i,\kappa_{M}=\kappa}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\left(\sum_{\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{M}:((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}},

where the first equality follows from the fact that
(f,g,\epsilon)
satisfies the constraints (
54
), the second equality follows from line (
55
), the third equality follows from algebra, the fourth equality follows from algebra and Claim
12
, and the fifth equality follows from our construction of
\lambda
on line (
56
).
Since
\lambda
is clearly nonnegative, our proof that
(\lambda,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
) is complete.

The remainder of the proof of Lemma
I.5
consists of bounding the objective value associated with
(\lambda,\epsilon)
in the linear optimization problem (
2
). To develop our bound, we establish the following key property about directed acyclic graph
\mathfrak{G}\equiv(\mathfrak{V},\mathfrak{E})
.

Claim 13

If
((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}
and
(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
, then
\kappa_{m}\geq\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
.

Proof I.8

Proof of Claim
13
.
Consider any path
((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}
that satisfies
(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
.
Our proof of Claim
13
follows from an induction argument, and the induction argument proceeds as follows.

In the base case, we let
m=1
. In this case, it follows from the fact that
((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}
that the inclusion
(1,i_{1},\kappa_{1})\in\mathfrak{V}
holds, and so it follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{V}
that
\kappa_{1}\in\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\}
. Thus, it follows from the facts that
\kappa_{1}\in\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\}
and
(1,i_{1},\kappa_{1})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)
that

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\in\begin{cases}\{r_{i_{1}}\},&\textnormal{if }i_{1}\in S,\\
\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\cap S\},&\textnormal{if }i_{1}\notin S.\end{cases}

(57)

Therefore, we observe that

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}r_{i_{1}},&\textnormal{if }i_{1}\in S,\\
\min_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\cap S}r_{j},&\textnormal{if }i_{1}\notin S\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{1}),

where the inequality follows from line (
57
) and the equality follows from Claim
12
and Lemma
5.10
. We have shown that
\kappa_{1}\geq\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{1})
, which concludes our proof of the base case.

Now choose any arbitrary
m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}
and assume by induction that the inequality
\kappa_{m^{\prime}}\geq\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m^{\prime}})
holds for all
m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
. In this case, it follows from the fact that
((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}
that the inclusion
((m-1,i_{m-1},\kappa_{m-1}),(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m}))\in\mathfrak{E}
holds, and so it follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{E}
that
\kappa_{m}\in\left\{\kappa_{m-1}\right\}\cup\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}
. Thus, it follows from the facts that
\kappa_{m}\in\left\{\kappa_{m-1}\right\}\cup\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}
and
(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)
that

\displaystyle\kappa_{m}\in\begin{cases}\{r_{i_{m}}\},&\textnormal{if }i_{m}\in S\text{ and }i_{m}\in\mathcal{B}_{m},\\
\{\kappa_{m-1}\},&\textnormal{if }i_{m}\in S\text{ and }i_{m}\notin\mathcal{B}_{m},\\
\{\kappa_{m-1}\}\cup\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap S\},&\textnormal{if }i_{m}\notin S.\end{cases}

(58)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\kappa_{m}

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}r_{i_{m}},&\textnormal{if }i_{m}\in S\text{ and }i_{m}\in\mathcal{B}_{m},\\
\kappa_{m-1},&\textnormal{if }i_{m}\in S\text{ and }i_{m}\notin\mathcal{B}_{m},\\
\min\left\{\kappa_{m-1},\min\limits_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap S}r_{j}\right\},&\textnormal{if }i_{m}\notin S\end{cases}

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}r_{i_{m}},&\textnormal{if }i_{m}\in S\text{ and }i_{m}\in\mathcal{B}_{m},\\
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1}),&\text{if }i_{m}\in S\text{ and }i_{m}\notin\mathcal{B}_{m},\\
\min\left\{\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m-1}),\min\limits_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\cap S}r_{j}\right\},&\textnormal{if }i_{m}\notin S\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m}),

where the first inequality follows from line (
58
), the second inequality follows from the induction hypothesis, and the equality follows from Claim
12
and Lemma
5.10
. We have thus shown that
\kappa_{m}\geq\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{m})
. This concludes our proof of the induction step, and thus our proof of Claim
13
is complete.

\halmos

Equipped with Claims
12
and
13
, we now develop the desired bound on the objective value associated with
(\lambda,\epsilon)
in the linear optimization problem (
2
):

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\left(\sum_{\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{M}:((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

(59)

\displaystyle=\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}

(60)

\displaystyle=\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M})\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}

(61)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)\;\forall m\in\mathcal{M}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M})\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:\exists m\in\mathcal{M}\textnormal{ such that }(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M})\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

(62)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)\;\forall m\in\mathcal{M}}\kappa_{M}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:\exists m\in\mathcal{M}\textnormal{ such that }(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M})\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

(63)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)\;\forall m\in\mathcal{M}}\kappa_{M}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:\exists m\in\mathcal{M}\textnormal{ such that }(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}r_{n}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

(64)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)\;\forall m\in\mathcal{M}}\kappa_{M}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}:(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}r_{n}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

(65)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)\;\forall m\in\mathcal{M}}\kappa_{M}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}r_{n}\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:i_{m}=i,\kappa_{m}=\kappa}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)

(66)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:(m,i_{m},\kappa_{m})\notin\mathfrak{N}(S)\;\forall m\in\mathcal{M}}\kappa_{M}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}g_{m,i,\kappa}

(67)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}}\kappa_{M}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}g_{m,i,\kappa}

(68)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa\sum_{((1,i_{1},\kappa_{1}),\ldots,(M,i_{M},\kappa_{M}))\in\mathscr{P}:i_{M}=i,\kappa_{M}=\kappa}\pi_{i_{1}\kappa_{1}\cdots i_{M}\kappa_{M}}\right)+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}g_{m,i,\kappa}

(69)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}g_{m,i,\kappa}.

(70)

Indeed, line (
59
) follows from our construction of
\lambda
on line (
56
). Line (
60
) follows from algebra and Claim
12
. Line (
61
) follows from Assumption
5.3
and Remark
5.1
. Line (
62
) follows from algebra. Line (
63
) follows from Claim
13
. Line (
64
) follows from the fact that
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cap S_{M})\leq r_{n}
for all
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
. Lines (
65
) and (
66
) follows from algebra. Line (
67
) follows from line (
55
). Lines (
68
) and (
69
) follow from algebra. Line (
70
) follows from line (
55
). Our proof of Lemma
I.5
is thus complete.

\halmos

I.5
Proof of Proposition
5.11

Let Assumption
5.3
hold, and consider any assortment
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
that satisfies
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
. It follows immediately from Lemma
I.2
from Appendix
I.4
that the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
2
) is greater than or equal to the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
6
). To show the other direction, consider any arbitrary feasible solution
(f,g,\epsilon)
for the linear optimization problem (
6
). It follows from Lemma
I.5
from Appendix
I.4
that there exists a vector
\lambda
such that
(\lambda,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
) and

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\leq\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{m,i,\kappa}+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}r_{n}g_{m,i,\kappa},

where the definition of the set
\mathfrak{N}(S)
can be found at the beginning of Appendix
I.4
.
In particular, it follows from the fact that
(f,g,\epsilon)
is feasible for the linear optimization problem (
6
) that the equality
g_{m,i,\kappa}=0
holds for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)
. Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{m,i,\kappa}+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}r_{n}0

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{m,i,\kappa}.

Since
(f,g,\epsilon)
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
6
) is greater than or equal to the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
2
). Our proof of Proposition
5.11
is thus complete.

\halmos

I.6
Proof of Theorem
5.12

Let Assumption
5.3
hold and
S_{1},\ldots,S_{M}\in\mathcal{S}
. It follows from the fact that
S_{1}\in\mathcal{S}
that
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
for all
S\in\mathcal{S}
. Therefore, it follows from Proposition
5.11
for each assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
that the worst-case expected revenue
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
is equal to the optimal objective value of the following linear optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{f,g,\epsilon}{\textnormal{minimize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}

(
6
)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}g_{m,i,\kappa}-\epsilon_{m,i}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}=0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}g_{M,i,\kappa}=1

\displaystyle\|\epsilon\|\leq\eta

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}},

where the definition of the set
\mathfrak{N}(S)
can be found in the beginning of Appendix
I.4
.

At a high level, our following analysis will consist of two main steps. First, we will use strong duality to transform the linear optimization problem (
6
) into a maximization problem. Second, we will introduce binary decision variables to represent the assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
. The key challenge in performing these two main steps is ensuring that the resulting mixed-integer optimization problem does not have any nonlinear terms. To see why this is a challenge, suppose for the sake of discussion that we introduced a binary decision variable
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
for each product
i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
that satisfies
x_{i}=1
if and only if product
i
is in the assortment
S
. Under this choice of binary decision variables, it is easy to verify using the definition of
\mathfrak{N}(S)
from the beginning of Appendix
I.4
that the constraints in the linear optimization problem (
6
) of the form

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}=0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)

will be satisfied if and only if

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq x_{j}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}\textnormal{ and }j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}:\kappa=r_{j}.

However, we observe that the binary decision variables
x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
appear on the right hand side of the above constraints. Therefore, if one added the above constraints into the linear optimization problem (
6
), then the dual of the linear optimization problem (
6
) would have an objective function in which dual decision variables are multiplied by the binary decision variables
x
.

To circumvent the aforementioned challenge and obtain a mixed-integer
linear
optimization reformulation of (
RO
), we will make use of the following Claim
14
. Specifically, the following Claim
14
shows that the constraints of the form
g_{m,i,\kappa}=0\;\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)
can be moved into the objective function of the linear optimization problem (
6
) without loss of generality. By using the following claim, we will subsequently be able to obtain a dual of the linear optimization problem (
6
) in which the binary decision variables
x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
are not multiplied by dual decision variables. The proof of the following Claim
14
follows from similar reasoning as the proof of Proposition
5.11
.

Claim 14

Consider any assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
, and let
\mathfrak{M}(S)\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathfrak{V}}}
be any vector that satisfies the inequality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)\geq r_{n}
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)
and satisfies the equality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)=0
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\setminus\mathfrak{N}(S)
. Then the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
6
) is equal to the optimal objective value of the following linear optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{f,g,\epsilon}{\textnormal{minimize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)g_{m,i,\kappa}

(71)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}g_{m,i,\kappa}-\epsilon_{m,i}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}g_{M,i,\kappa}=1

\displaystyle\|\epsilon\|\leq\eta

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}.

Proof I.9

Proof of Claim
14
. Consider any assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
, and let
\mathfrak{M}(S)\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathfrak{V}}}
be any vector that satisfies the inequality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)\geq r_{n}
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)
and satisfies the equality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)=0
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\setminus\mathfrak{N}(S)
.
We readily observe that the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
71
) is a lower bound on the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
6
), since the constraints of the form
g_{m,i,\kappa}=0\;\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)
from the linear optimization problem (
6
) have been relaxed and since the equality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)=0
holds for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\setminus\mathfrak{N}(S)
. To show the other direction, consider any arbitrary feasible solution
(f,g,\epsilon)
for the linear optimization problem (
71
). Because Assumption
5.3
holds and
S_{1}\cap S\neq\emptyset
, it follows from Lemma
I.5
from Appendix
I.4
that there exists a vector
\lambda
such that
(\lambda,\epsilon)
is a feasible solution for the linear optimization problem (
2
) and

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{m,i,\kappa}+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)}r_{n}g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{m,i,\kappa}+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\mathfrak{M}(S)g_{m,i,\kappa},

where the first inequality follows from Lemma
I.5
and the second inequality follows from our construction of the vector
\mathfrak{M}(S)
. Since
(f,g,\epsilon)
was chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
2
) is a lower bound on the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
71
). Since we showed in Proposition
5.11
that the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
71
) is equal to the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
6
), we conclude that the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
6
) is a lower bound on the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
71
). Our proof of Claim
14
is thus complete.

\halmos

To simplify our exposition, the remainder of the proof of Theorem
5.12
focuses on the case where
\eta=0
. Indeed, it is easy to see that our reformulations of the robust optimization problem (
RO
) for the case of
\eta=0
can be readily extended to the case of
\eta>0
by introducing
\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n,M))
auxiliary decision variables and auxiliary linear constraints in the dual problem. In the case of
\eta=0
, we observe that the linear optimization problem (
71
) can be rewritten equivalently as

\displaystyle\underset{f,g}{\textnormal{minimize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}+\sum_{(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)g_{m,i,\kappa}

(
71
)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}g_{m,i,\kappa}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}.

We observe from strong duality that the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
71
) is thus equal to the optimal objective value of the following linear optimization problem (
72
) for every assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
and every choice of
\mathfrak{M}(S)\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathfrak{V}}}
that satisfies the inequality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)\geq r_{n}
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)
and satisfies the equality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)=0
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\setminus\mathfrak{N}(S)
:

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M},i\in S_{m}}v_{m,i}\alpha_{m,i}

(72)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\alpha_{m,i}-\beta_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}\right\}-\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa\mathbb{I}\left\{m=M\right\}+\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}

\displaystyle\beta_{m,i,\kappa}+\gamma_{m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\leq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle\alpha_{m,i}\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\beta_{m,i,\kappa},\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}.

Consequently, if
\mathfrak{M}(\cdot)
satisfies the inequality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)\geq r_{n}
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)
and satisfies the equality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)=0
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\setminus\mathfrak{N}(S)
and all assortments
S\in\mathcal{S}
, then

(
RO
)

\displaystyle=\max_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\;\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)

\displaystyle=\underset{S\in\mathcal{S}}{\textnormal{max}}\left[\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}{\textnormal{maximize}}&&\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M},i\in S_{m}}v_{m,i}\alpha_{m,i}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\alpha_{m,i}-\beta_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}\right\}-\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}\right\}\\
&&&\hskip 8.50012pt\leq\kappa\mathbb{I}\left\{m=M\right\}+\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)&&\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\\
&&&\beta_{m,i,\kappa}+\gamma_{m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\leq 0&&\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}\\
&&&\alpha_{m,i}\in\mathbb{R}&&\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}\\
&&&\beta_{m,i,\kappa},\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}&&\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\end{aligned}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{S,\alpha,\beta,\gamma}{\textnormal{maximize}}&&\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M},i\in S_{m}}v_{m,i}\alpha_{m,i}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\alpha_{m,i}-\beta_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}\right\}-\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}\right\}\\
&&&\hskip 8.50012pt\leq\kappa\mathbb{I}\left\{m=M\right\}+\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)&&\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\\
&&&\beta_{m,i,\kappa}+\gamma_{m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\leq 0&&\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}\\
&&&\alpha_{m,i}\in\mathbb{R}&&\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}\\
&&&\beta_{m,i,\kappa},\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}&&\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\\
&&&S\in\mathcal{S}\end{aligned}\right],

(73)

where the first equality follows from the definition of the robust optimization problem (
RO
), the second equality follows from Claim
14
(which implies that
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
is equal to the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
72
) for all assortments
S\in\mathcal{S}
), and the third equality follows from algebra.

To transform the maximization problem in line (
73
) into a mixed-integer optimization problem, we introduce a binary decision variable
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
for each product
i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
that satisfies
x_{i}=1
if and only if product
i
is in the assortment
S
. Under this equivalence between an assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
and the binary decision variables
x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
, it follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{N}(\cdot)
that the following equality holds for all assortments
S\in\mathcal{S}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}(S)\triangleq\left\{(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:\begin{gathered}\left[x_{i}=1\textnormal{ and }i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}\right]\\
\textnormal{ or }\left[x_{i}=1\textnormal{ and }i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}\right]\\
\textnormal{ or }\left[\textnormal{there exists }j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ such that }\kappa=r_{j}\textnormal{ and }x_{j}=0\right]\end{gathered}\right\}.

Moreover, for each assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
and each
(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}
, let us define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)\triangleq r_{n}x_{i}\mathbb{I}\left\{i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}\right\}+r_{n}x_{i}\mathbb{I}\left\{i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\}\right\}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}}\mathbb{I}\left\{\kappa=r_{j}\right\}r_{n}(1-x_{j})

(77)

It follows immediately from line (
I.6
) and from the equivalence between each assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
and the binary decision variables
x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
that the function
\mathfrak{M}(\cdot)
defined above satisfies the inequality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)\geq r_{n}
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)
and satisfies the equality
\mathfrak{M}_{m,i,\kappa}(S)=0
for all
(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\setminus\mathfrak{N}(S)
. Thus, it follows from (
73
) that the robust optimization problem (
RO
) is equivalent to

,\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{x,\alpha,\beta,\gamma}{\textnormal{maximize}}&&\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M},i\in S_{m}}v_{m,i}\alpha_{m,i}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\alpha_{m,i}-\beta_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}\right\}-\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}\right\}\\
&&&\quad\leq\kappa\mathbb{I}\left\{m=M\right\}\\
&&&\quad\quad+r_{n}x_{i}\mathbb{I}\left\{i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}\right\}\\
&&&\quad\quad+r_{n}x_{i}\mathbb{I}\left\{i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\}\right\}\\
&&&\quad\quad+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}}\mathbb{I}\left\{\kappa=r_{j}\right\}r_{n}(1-x_{j})&&\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\\
&&&\beta_{m,i,\kappa}+\gamma_{m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\leq 0&&\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}\\
&&&x_{0}=1\\
&&&\alpha_{m,i}\in\mathbb{R}&&\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}\\
&&&\beta_{m,i,\kappa},\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}&&\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}\\
&&&x_{i}\in\{0,1\}&&\forall i\in\mathcal{N}_{0},\end{aligned}

(78)

where we observe that the mixed-integer optimization problem (
78
) is a reformulation of (
73
) in which the assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
is represented by binary decision variables
x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
that satisfy
x_{i}=1
if and only if
i\in S
and in which
\mathfrak{M}(\cdot)
is implemented using the definition from line (
77
). We note that the constraint
x_{0}=1
is enforced in the mixed-integer optimization problem (
78
) because
S\in\mathcal{S}
if and only if
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
and
0\in S
. This completes our mixed-integer optimization reformulation of the robust optimization problem (
RO
) in the case of
\eta=0
. Since a similar mixed-integer optimization reformulation can be obtained for the case of
\eta>0
, our proof of Theorem
5.12
is complete.

\halmos

Appendix J
Proofs of Technical Results from §
6.1

J.1
Proof of Theorem
6.1

Because the proof of Theorem
6.1
closely resembles the proof of Theorem
5.5
, we focus in the present appendix on the main areas of distinction.

J.1.1
Characterization of Optimal Assortments.

We begin our proof of Theorem
6.1
by characterizing the structure of optimal assortments for the optimistic optimization problem (
OO
). To do this, we introduce an optimistic version of Definition
4.12
from §
4.4
. Indeed, for each tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
, let
\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
be defined as the maximum revenue among the products in the assortment
S
that can be the most preferred product in
S
under a ranking that corresponds to the tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})
.

Definition J.1
