\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\triangleq\max\limits_{i\in S:\;\cap_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{D}_{i_{m}}(S_{m})\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}(S)\neq\emptyset}r_{i}
.

Equipped with the above notation, the following result shows that the best-case expected revenue of any fixed assortment can be reformulated as a linear optimization problem. The proof of the following Proposition
J.2
follows from identical reasoning as Proposition
4.13
and is thus omitted.

Proposition J.2

For each
S\in\mathcal{S}
,
\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
is equal to the optimal objective value of the following linear optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{\lambda,\epsilon}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}

(79)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}:\;i_{m}=i}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}-\epsilon_{m,i}=v_{m,i}\quad\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}}\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}=1

\displaystyle\|\epsilon\|\leq\eta

\displaystyle\lambda_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}\geq 0\quad\forall(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}.

Following identical reasoning and using the same notation as developed in Appendix
B.2
, we obtain the following graphical interpretation of the quantity
\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
. In particular, the proof of the following Proposition
J.3
follows from identical reasoning as Proposition
B.11
and is thus omitted.

Proposition J.3

For all
S\in\mathcal{S}
and
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
,

\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)=\max_{i\in S\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)}r_{i}.

Equipped with the graphical interpretation of
\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
provided by Proposition
J.3
, we derive the following optimistic version of Proposition
4.16
from §
4.4
:

Proposition J.4

Let
S\in\mathcal{S}
and
i\notin S
. If there exists
i^{*}\in\mathcal{S}
that satisfies
r_{i^{*}}>r_{i}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}
, then
\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\geq\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cup\{i\})
for each
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
.

Proof J.5

Proof.
We begin our proof of Proposition
J.4
by developing an intermediary result, denoted below by Claim
15
, that will allow us to compare the values of
\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
and
\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cup\{i\})
for every assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
and every product
i
which is not in the assortment. The proof of the following Claim
15
, which uses the graphical interpretation of the quantity
\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
from Proposition
J.4
, follows from identical reasoning as the proof of Claim
1
from Appendix
B.3
and is thus omitted.

Claim 15

For all
S\in\mathcal{S}
,
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
, and
i\notin S
,

\displaystyle\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cup\{i\})=\begin{cases}\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S),&\textnormal{if }i\notin\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S),\\
\max\left\{\max\limits_{j\in S\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\cap\left\{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:i\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j^{\prime}\right\}}r_{j},r_{i}\right\},&\textnormal{if }i\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S).\end{cases}

Using the above intermediary result, we now complete the proof of Proposition
J.4
. Indeed, consider any assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
and any product
i\notin S
. Suppose that there exists a product
i^{*}\in S
which satisfies
r_{i^{*}}>r_{i}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}
. For each tuple of products
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
, we have two cases to consider:

•

Case 1:
Suppose that
i\notin\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
. In this case, it follows immediately from Claim
15
that

\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cup\{i\})=\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S),

and so the inequality
\rho_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\leq\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cup\{i\})
holds when
i\notin\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
.

•

Case 2:
Suppose that
i\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
.

In this case, we begin by showing that
i_{m}\notin S
for each
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
. Indeed, consider any past assortment
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
. On one hand, if
i=i_{m}
, then it follows immediately from the fact that
i\notin S
that
i_{m}\notin S
. On the other hand, if
i\neq i_{m}
, then it also must be the case that
i_{m}\notin S
, else we would have a contradiction with the fact that
i\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
and the fact that there is, by our construction of
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
, a directed edge from vertex
i
to vertex
i_{m}
. We have thus shown that
i_{m}\notin S
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
.

We next show that
i_{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
. Indeed, consider any arbitrary
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
. On one hand, if
i_{m}=i
, then the statement
i_{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
follows immediately from the fact that
i\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
. On the other hand, if
i_{m}\neq i
, then it follows from the fact that
i\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
and the fact that there is a directed edge from vertex
i
to vertex
i_{m}
that there must not be a directed path from vertex
i_{m}
to a vertex
i_{m^{\prime}}
that satisfies
i_{m^{\prime}}\in S
for any
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
. We have thus shown that
i_{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
.

Using the above results, we now prove that
i^{*}\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
. Indeed, we have shown in the above results that
i_{m}\notin S
and
i_{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
. Therefore, it follows from the supposition that
\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}
that
i_{m}\notin S
and
i_{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}
. Since we have supposed that
i^{*}\in S
, it follows from the fact that
i_{m}\notin S
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}
that
i^{*}\neq i_{m}
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}
. Moreover, it follows from the construction of
\mathcal{G}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}
that all of the outgoing edges from vertex
i^{*}
are incoming edges to vertices
i_{m}
for
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}
. Therefore, it follows from the fact that
i_{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}
that
i^{*}\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
.

We now prove the desired result for Case 2. First, we observe that

\displaystyle\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)=\max_{j\in S\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)}r_{j}\geq r_{i^{*}},

(80)

where the equality follows from Proposition
J.3
and the inequality follows from our supposition that
i^{*}\in S
and because we have shown that
i^{*}\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
.
Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cup\{i\})

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\max\limits_{j\in S\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\cap\{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}:i\nprec_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}j^{\prime}\}}r_{j},r_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\max\limits_{j\in S\cap\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)}r_{j},r_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S),r_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S),

where the first equality follows from Claim
1
and the supposition of Case 2 that
i\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)
, the inequality follows algebra, the second equality follows from Proposition
J.3
, and the final equality holds because of our supposition that
r_{i^{*}}>r_{i}
and because of line (
80
), which showed that
\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\geq r_{i^{*}}
. This concludes the proof of Case 2.

In both of the above two cases, we showed that
\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)\geq\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\cup\{i\})
, and so our proof of Proposition
J.4
is complete.

\halmos

In view of the above Proposition
J.4
,
we proceed to develop our main result of Appendix
J.1.1
, which is a characterization of the structure of optimal assortments for the optimistic optimization problem (
OO
). To this end, we introduce the following new collection of assortments:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{OO}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{S\in\mathcal{S}:\;\textnormal{if }i^{*}\in S,\;0<r_{i}<r_{i^{*}},\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i},\textnormal{ then }i\notin S\right\}.

Our main result is the following:

Theorem J.6

There exists an assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
that is optimal for (
OO
).

Proof J.7

Proof.
Consider any arbitrary assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
. For this assortment, we define a new assortment as

S^{\prime}\triangleq S\setminus\left\{i\in\mathcal{N}:\text{there exists }i^{*}\in S\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}\textnormal{ and }r_{i^{*}}>r_{i}\right\}.

We first show that this new assortment
S^{\prime}
is an element of the collection
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
. Indeed, it follows from the fact that
S\in\mathcal{S}
that
0\in S^{\prime}
, which implies that
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
. Moreover, for every
i^{*}\in S^{\prime}
, it follows from the construction of
S^{\prime}
that there does not exist
i\in S\setminus\{0\}
that satisfies
r_{i}<r_{i^{*}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}
. We thus conclude that
S^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
.

We next show for each product
i\in S^{\prime}\setminus S
that there must exist a product
i^{*}\in S^{\prime}
that satisfies
r_{i}<r_{i^{*}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}
. Indeed, consider any product
i\in S^{\prime}\setminus S
. It follows from the construction of
S^{\prime}
that there exists a product
i^{\prime}\in S
that satisfies
\mathcal{M}_{i^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}
and
r_{i}<r_{i^{\prime}}
. Let
i^{*}\triangleq\argmax_{i^{\prime}\in S:\mathcal{M}_{i^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}\text{ and }r_{i}<r_{i^{\prime}}}r_{i^{\prime}}
be the product with the highest revenue among all products
i^{\prime}
that satisfy
\mathcal{M}_{i^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}
and
r_{i}<r_{i^{\prime}}
. It follows from the definition of
i^{*}
that there does not exist any products
i^{\prime\prime}\in S
that satisfy
\mathcal{M}_{i^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}
and
r_{i^{\prime\prime}}>r_{i^{*}}
. Therefore, we conclude that the product
i^{*}
is an element of
S^{\prime}
.

Finally, let
\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\nu}\}\triangleq S\setminus S^{\prime}
denote the products that have been removed from the assortment
S
. We observe for each tuple of products
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})\in\mathcal{L}
that

\displaystyle\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S)+\sum_{\iota=1}^{\nu}\left(\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\setminus\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\iota}\})-\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S\setminus\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\iota-1}\})\right)\leq\xi_{i_{1}\cdots i_{M}}(S).

Indeed, the equality follows from algebra. The inequality follows from Proposition
J.4
together with the fact that for each
\iota\in\{1,\ldots,\nu\}
, there exists a product
i^{*}\in S^{\prime}
that satisfies
r_{j_{\iota}}<r_{i^{*}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{j_{\iota}}
. Since the assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
was chosen arbitrarily, our proof of Theorem
J.6
follows from Proposition
J.2
.

\halmos

J.1.2
Upper Bound.

As the second step of our proof of Theorem
6.1
, we develop an upper bound on the cardinality of the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
that we showed in Theorem
J.6
contains an optimal assortment for the optimistic optimization problem (
OO
).

Lemma J.8

|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}|\leq(n+1)^{2^{M}}
.

Proof J.9

Proof.
We readily observe that the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
is a subset of

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}\triangleq\left\{S\in\mathcal{S}:\;\textnormal{if }i^{*}\in S,\;0<r_{i}<r_{i^{*}},\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}=\mathcal{M}_{i},\textnormal{ then }i\notin S\right\},

where we recall from the beginning of §
4
that
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is defined as the subset of the past assortments
\mathcal{M}\equiv\{1,\ldots,M\}
that offered product
i
.
For each subset of past assortments
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
, let the products that are offered only in the assortments in
\mathcal{C}
(excluding the no-purchase option) be denoted by

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{i\in\mathcal{N}:\mathcal{M}_{i}=\mathcal{C}\right\}.

Equipped with the above definitions, we observe that
\{\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C}):\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{M}\}
is the collection of subsets of products that always appear together in the past assortments, and we readily observe that
|\{\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C}):\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{M}\}|=2^{M}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}|

\displaystyle\leq|\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}|\leq\prod_{\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{M}}\left(\left|\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})\right|+1\right)\leq(n+1)^{2^{M}}.

(81)

Indeed, the first inequality on line (
81
) holds because the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
is a subset of the collection of assortments
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
. To see why the second inequality on line (
81
) holds, consider any arbitrary subset of past assortments
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
, and let the products in
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})
be indexed in ascending order by revenue; that is, let the products that comprise
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})
be denoted by
i_{1}^{\mathcal{C}},\ldots,i_{|\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})|}^{\mathcal{C}}
, where
r_{i_{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}<\cdots<r_{i_{|\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})|}^{\mathcal{C}}}
. Then, it follows from the definition of the collection
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
that every assortment
S\in\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
must satisfy the condition
\left|\left\{i^{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\ldots,i^{\mathcal{C}}_{|\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})|}\right\}\cap S\right|\leq 1
.
Since
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
was chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}\subseteq\left\{\{0\}\cup\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{C}}:\;\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{C}}\in\left\{\emptyset,\left\{i_{1}^{\mathcal{C}}\right\},\ldots,\left\{i_{|\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})|}^{\mathcal{C}}\right\}\right\}\right\},

which proves that the second inequality on line (
81
) holds. The third inequality on line (
81
) follows from the fact that
|\{\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C}):\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{M}\}|=2^{M}
and from the fact that
|\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})|\leq n
for every
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
. We have thus proven that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}|
is at most
(n+1)^{2^{M}}
, which concludes our proof of Lemma
J.8
.
\halmos

J.1.3
Algorithm for Constructing
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
.

As the third step of our proof of Theorem
6.1
, we develop an efficient algorithm for constructing the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
that contains an optimal assortment for the optimistic optimization problem (
OO
). Specifically, our main result of Appendix
J.1.3
is the following lemma:

Lemma J.10

The collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
can be constructed in
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}(M+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}|))
time.

Proof J.11

Proof.

We begin by developing an algorithm for constructing a directed graph, denoted by
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
, in which the set of vertices in the directed graph is defined as
\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\triangleq\mathcal{N}
and the set of directed edges is defined as
\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\triangleq\{(i^{*},i)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N}:\;r_{i^{*}}>r_{i}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}\}
.
This directed graph has a natural correspondence with the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
, as we readily observe that an assortment satisfies
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
if and only if [
0\in S
] and [we have that
i\notin S
whenever there exists a product
i^{*}\in S\setminus\{0\}
and a directed edge
(i^{*},i)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}
]. It is easy to see that this directed graph is acyclic and has the property that a vertex
j\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
is reachable from a vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
if and only if there is a directed edge
(i,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}
from vertex
i
to vertex
j
.
A directed acyclic graph which has the aforementioned property for each pair of vertices is referred to as a
transitive closure
.

Construct-
{\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}}(\mathscr{M},r)

Inputs
:

•

The collection of past assortments,
\mathscr{M}\equiv\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{M}\}
.

•

The revenues of the products,
r\equiv(r_{0},r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
.

Output
:

•

\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
, where
\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv\mathcal{N}
and
\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv\{(i^{*},i)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N}:\;r_{i^{*}}>r_{i}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}\}
.

Procedure
:

1.

Initialize the vertex set
\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\leftarrow\emptyset
and edge set
\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\leftarrow\emptyset
.

2.

For each product
i\in\mathcal{N}^{\textnormal{OO}}
:

(a)

Update
\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\leftarrow\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\cup\{i\}
.

(b)

Construct the set
\mathcal{M}_{i}
of past assortments which offered product
i
.

3.

For each pair of products
(i^{*},i)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N}
:

(a)

If
r_{i^{*}}>r_{i}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}
:

i.

Update
\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\leftarrow\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\cup\{(i^{*},i)\}

4.

Output
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
and terminate.

Algorithm 5
:
A procedure for constructing the directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}
.

Our algorithm for constructing the directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}
that is a transitive closure is presented in Algorithm
5
. In the algorithm, we first iterate over each product
i\in\mathcal{N}
and construct the corresponding set
\mathcal{M}_{i}
of past assortments which offered that product. We then iterate over each pair of products
(i,i^{*})\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N}
and check whether
r_{i^{*}}>r_{i}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{i}
. It follows from identical reasoning as that given for Algorithm
1
in Appendix
D.3
that Algorithm
5
has a total computation time of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}M)
.

We next describe our algorithm for constructing the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
from the directed graph
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}
. This algorithm is denoted by
Construct-
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}(\mathscr{M},r)
and is found in Algorithm
6
. In this algorithm, we first use Algorithm
5
to construct the directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
, and then we invoke a recursive subroutine denoted by
RecursiveStep-OO
(\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}})
in Algorithm
7
. The goal of the recursive subroutine is to take as an input a generic directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
, and for that graph, output the collection of subsets of vertices
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}:\text{ if $i\in S$ and $(i,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}$, then $j\notin S$}\}
. Algorithm
6
concludes by adding the no-purchase option
0
into each of the subsets of vertices from
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}
. The correctness of Algorithm
6
follows immediately from our earlier observation that an assortment satisfies
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
if and only if [
0\in S
] and [we have that
i\notin S
whenever there exists a product
i^{*}\in S\setminus\{0\}
and a directed edge
(i^{*},i)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}
].

At a high level, the recursive subroutine in Algorithm
7
is comprised of two cases. The base case of the subroutine is when the graph has no vertices, in which case it is clear that
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}=\{\emptyset\}
. If we are not in the base case, then the aim of the recursive subroutine is to construct the collections
\left\{S\in\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}:i\notin S\right\}
and
\left\{S\in\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}:i\in S\right\}
for a chosen vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
and output the union of these two collections. The construction of the collection
\left\{S\in\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}:i\notin S\right\}
takes place on lines (
2b
)-(
2c
) of Algorithm
7
, and the construction of the collection
\left\{S\in\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}:i\in S\right\}
takes place on lines (
2d
)-(
2f
) of Algorithm
7
.

Up to this point, we have established that Algorithm
5
is correct (that is, it delivers the desired output for any valid input), and we have established that Algorithm
6
is correct under the assumption that Algorithm
7
is correct. Therefore, it remains for us to prove that the recursive subroutine in Algorithm
7
is correct. To prove the correctness of the recursive subroutine, we will make use of four intermediary claims, which are denoted below by Claims
16
-
19
. The purpose of the first two intermediary claims, denoted by Claims
16
and
17
, is to show that the graphs
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}})
and
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}})
constructed on lines (
2b
) and (
2d
) of Algorithm
7
are directed acyclic graphs that are transitive closures, which implies that
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}}
are valid inputs on lines (
2c
) and (
2e
) of Algorithm
7
. The purpose of the second two intermediary claims, denoted by Claims
18
and
19
, is to show that the union of the two collections
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}
and
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
constructed on lines (
2c
) and (
2f
) of Algorithm
7
provides the desired output on line (
2g
) of Algorithm
7
.

Construct-
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}(\mathscr{M},r)

Inputs
:

•

The collection of past assortments,
\mathscr{M}\equiv\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{M}\}
.

•

The revenues of the products,
r\equiv(r_{0},r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
.

Output
:

•

The collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
corresponding to the collection of past assortments
\mathscr{M}
and the revenues
r
.

Procedure
:

1.

Construct the directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\leftarrow\textsc{Construct-${\mathscr{G}}^{\textnormal{OO}}(\mathscr{M},r)$}
.

2.

Compute the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}\leftarrow\textsc{RecursiveStep-OO$(\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}})$}
.

3.

Output
\{S\cup\{0\}:S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}\}
and terminate.

Algorithm 6
:
A procedure for constructing
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
.

RecursiveStep-OO
(\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}})

Inputs
:

•

A directed acyclic graph
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
that is a transitive closure.

Output
:

•

The collection
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}:\text{ if $i\in S$ and $(i,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}$, then $j\notin S$}\}
.

Procedure
:

1.

If
\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}=\emptyset
:

(a)

Output the collection
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv\{\emptyset\}
and terminate.

2.

Otherwise:

(a)

Choose any vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
.

(b)

Create a copy of
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
in which the vertex
i
and its incoming and outgoing edges are removed. Denote this new graph by
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}})
.

(c)

Compute the collection
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}\leftarrow\textsc{RecursiveStep-OO$(\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}})$}
.

(d)

Create a copy of
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
in which the vertices
\{i\}\cup\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\}\cup\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\}
and the incoming and outgoing edges of these vertices are removed. Denote this new graph by
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}})
.

(e)

Compute the collection
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}}\leftarrow\textsc{RecursiveStep$(\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}})$}
.

(f)

Compute the collection
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\leftarrow\{S\cup\{i\}:S\in\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}}\}
.

(g)

Output the collection
\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}\cup\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
and terminate.

Algorithm 7
:
A recursive subroutine which is used in Algorithm
6
.

Claim 16

Let
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. For notational convenience, let
\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}}
denote the set of incoming and outgoing edges from each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
. Then for each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
, we have that
\tilde{\mathscr{G}}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\{i\},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}})
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure.

Claim 17

Let
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. For notational convenience, let
\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}}
denote the set of incoming and outgoing edges from each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
. Then for each subset of vertices
\mathscr{B}^{\textnormal{OO}}\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
, we have that
\tilde{\mathscr{G}}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\mathscr{B}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus(\cup_{i\in\mathscr{B}^{\textnormal{OO}}}\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}}))
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure.

Claim 18

Let
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. For notational convenience, let
\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}}
denote the set of incoming and outgoing edges from each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
. For each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
,

\displaystyle\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\{i\}:\textnormal{ if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}$, then $j\notin S$}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\{i\}:\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}},\textnormal{ then }j\notin S\right\}.

Claim 19

Let
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
be a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure. For notational convenience, let
\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}}
denote the set of incoming and outgoing edges from each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
. For each vertex
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
,

\displaystyle\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}:\left[i\in S\right]\textnormal{ and }\left[\textnormal{if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}$, then $j\notin S$}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &S^{\prime}\cup\{i\}:\\
&\quad S^{\prime}\in\left\{\begin{aligned} &S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\left(\{i\}\cup\left\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\right\}\cup\left\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\right\}\right):\\
&\quad\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\left(\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{OO}}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{OO}}\right),\textnormal{ then }j\in S\end{aligned}\right\}\end{aligned}\right\}.

Proof J.12

Proof. The proofs of Claims

,

,

, and

follows from identical reasoning as the proofs of Claims

Using the above intermediary claims, we now prove the correctness of the recursive subroutine in Algorithm
7
. Indeed, it is clear that the recursive subroutine yields the correct output in the base case where
\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}=\emptyset
. Next, let us assume by induction that the recursive subroutine yields the correct output for all valid input graphs with up to
p-1
vertices, and consider any valid input
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
for which the number of vertices is
|\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}|=p
. Let
i\in\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}
denote the vertex from this graph which is chosen in line (
2a
) of Algorithm
3
, where the existence of such a vertex follows from the fact that we are in the case where
\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\neq\emptyset
. It follows immediately from Claim
3
that the graphs
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}})
and
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}})
constructed on lines (
2b
) and (
2d
) of Algorithm
7
are directed acyclic graphs which are transitive closures, which implies that these graphs are valid inputs to Algorithm
7
in lines (
2c
) and (
2e
). Therefore, it follows from the induction hypothesis and lines (
2c
), (
2e
), and (
2f
) of Algorithm
7
that

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\{i\}:\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}},\textnormal{ then }j\notin S\right\}

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &S^{\prime}\cup\{i\}:\\
&\quad S^{\prime}\in\left\{\begin{aligned} &S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\left(\{i\}\cup\left\{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\right\}\cup\left\{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\right\}\right):\\
&\quad\textnormal{if }k\in S\textnormal{ and }(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\left(\mathscr{E}_{i}^{\textnormal{OO}}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(i,\ell)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{OO}}\cup\bigcup_{\ell:(\ell,i)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}}\mathscr{E}_{\ell}^{\textnormal{OO}}\right),\textnormal{ then }j\in S\end{aligned}\right\}\end{aligned}\right\}

where the induction hypothesis can be applied because
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}})
and
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}})
are valid inputs to Algorithm
7
and because
|\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}|\leq p-1
and
|\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime}}|\leq p-1
. Therefore, it follows from Claims
18
and
19
that

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}\setminus\{i\}:\textnormal{ if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}$, then $j\notin S$}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}:\left[i\in S\right]\textnormal{ and }\left[\textnormal{if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}$, then $j\notin S$}\right]\right\},

which proves that the output of Algorithm
7
is

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime}}\cup\mathscr{A}^{\textnormal{OO}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=\left\{S\subseteq\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}:\textnormal{ if $k\in S$ and $(k,j)\in\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}}$, then $j\notin S$}\right\}.

This completes our proof of the correctness of Algorithm
7
.

We conclude Appendix
J.1.3
by analyzing the computation time of Algorithm

. Indeed, we recall that the computation time required for line (

) in Algorithm

is
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}M)
. In what follows, we assume that all directed graphs are stored as adjacency lists. Under this assumption, our analysis of the computation time for line (

) in Algorithm

is split into the following two intermediary claims, denoted by Claim

and

. In our first intermediary claim, presented below as Claim

, we establish the computation time required for lines (

), (

), (

), and (

) of Algorithm

. The proof of the following Claim

follows from similar reasoning as the proof of Claim

from Appendix

and is thus omitted.

Claim 20

If
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure with
|\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}|\geq 1
, then lines (

), (

), and (

In our second intermediary claim, presented below as Claim

, we use Claim

to establish the computation time for Algorithm

for any valid input. The proof of the following Claim

from Appendix

and is thus omitted.

Claim 21

If
\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})
is a directed acyclic graph that is a transitive closure with
|\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}|\geq 1
, then the computation time for Algorithm

is
\mathcal{O}(|\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}}|^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep-OO}(\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}})\right|)
.

Our analysis of the computation time for line (

) of Algorithm

follows readily from Claim

\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}}\equiv(\mathscr{V}^{\textnormal{OO}},\mathscr{E}^{\textnormal{OO}})

. Therefore, it follows from Claim

requires
\mathcal{O}((n+1)^{2}\times\left|\textsc{RecursiveStep-OO}(\mathscr{G}^{\textnormal{OO}})\right|)=\mathcal{O}(n^{2}|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}|)
computation time. We have shown that the total computation time for Algorithm

is

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathcal{O}(n^{2}M)}_{\eqref{step:construct_S:1:optimistic}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{O}(n^{2}|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}|)}_{\eqref{step:construct_S:2:optimistic}}=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}(M+|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}|)\right).

\halmos

J.1.4
Computation time for solving (

).

We conclude our proof of Theorem

by combining the intermediary results from Appendices

,

, and

. Indeed, we showed in Theorem
J.6
in Appendix

that there exists an optimal solution for the optimistic optimization problem (

) that is an element of the collection of assortments
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
. Moreover, we showed in Lemma
J.8
in Appendix
J.1.2
that
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}|
is upper bounded by a polynomial in the number of products
n
for any fixed number of past assortments
M
, and so it follows from Lemma

in Appendix
J.1.3
that the collection
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
can be constructed in computation time that is polynomial in the number of products
n
for any fixed number of past assortments
M
. Finally, for each assortment
S\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\textnormal{OO}}
, it follows from Proposition
J.2
in Appendix
J.1.1

that the best-case expected revenue
\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
can be computed by solving a linear optimization problem with numbers of decision variables and constraints that scale as a polynomial in the number of products
n
for any fixed number of past assortments
M
.
21
21

21

It follows identical reasoning as Lemmas
5.6
and

from §

that the linear optimization reformulation of the best-case expected revenue
\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
can be constructed in time that is polynomial in the number of products

for any fixed number of past assortments

.
We thus conclude that (
OO
) can be solved in time that is polynomial in the number of products

for any fixed number of past assortments

.

J.2
Proof of Theorem
6.2

Let Assumption
5.3
hold, and let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{M}\in\mathcal{S}
. To develop our reformulation of the objective function of the optimization problem (
OO
), we will utilize the following intermediary claim:

Claim 22

For each
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
that satisfies
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
,
\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
is equal to the optimal objective value of the following linear optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{f,g,\epsilon}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}

(82)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\textnormal{ same constraints as \eqref{prob:lp_reform_nested}}

Proof J.13

Proof of Claim
22
.
Consider any assortment
S\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{0}
that satisfies
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
. For each
i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
, let
\bar{r}_{i}\triangleq r_{n}-r_{i}
. We observe that

\displaystyle\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)

\displaystyle=\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}}r_{i}\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}_{i}(S)

\displaystyle=r_{n}+\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}}(r_{i}-r_{n})\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}_{i}(S)

\displaystyle=r_{n}-\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}}(r_{n}-r_{i})\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}_{i}(S)

\displaystyle=r_{n}-\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}}\bar{r}_{i}\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}_{i}(S).

(83)

Indeed, the first equality follows from the definition of the predicted expected revenue function
\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(\cdot)
from §
2
. The second equality follows from algebra and from the fact that
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}}\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}_{i}(S)=1
for all
\lambda\in\Delta_{\Sigma}
. The third equality follows from algebra. The fourth equality follows from the definition of the vector
\bar{r}
.

We observe that the optimization problem
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}}\bar{r}_{i}\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}_{i}(S)
is the same as the optimization problem
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}}{r}_{i}\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}_{i}(S)
, except with the revenue
r_{i}
for each product
i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
having been changed to
\bar{r}_{i}
. In particular, we observe that the vector
\bar{r}
satisfies
0=\bar{r}_{n}<\bar{r}_{n-1}<\cdots<\bar{r}_{1}<\bar{r}_{0}
. Thus, with a suitable reindexing of the products, it follows readily from Assumption
5.3
, from the fact that
S\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
, and from Proposition
5.11
that
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}}\bar{r}_{i}\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}_{i}(S)
is equal to the optimal objective value of the following linear optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{f,g,\epsilon}{\textnormal{minimize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}(r_{n}-\kappa)g_{M,i,\kappa}

(84)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\textnormal{ same constraints as \eqref{prob:lp_reform_nested}}

Combining the above analysis, we conclude that

\displaystyle\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)

\displaystyle=r_{n}-\left[\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{f,g,\epsilon}{\textnormal{minimize}}&&\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}(r_{n}-\kappa)g_{M,i,\kappa}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\textnormal{ same constraints as \eqref{prob:lp_reform_nested}}\end{aligned}\right]

\displaystyle=r_{n}+\left[\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{f,g,\epsilon}{\textnormal{maximize}}&&\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}(\kappa-r_{n})g_{M,i,\kappa}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\textnormal{ same constraints as \eqref{prob:lp_reform_nested}}\end{aligned}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{aligned} &\;\underset{f,g,\epsilon}{\textnormal{maximize}}&&\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\textnormal{ same constraints as \eqref{prob:lp_reform_nested}}\end{aligned}\right],

where the first equality follows from line (
83
) and from the fact that
\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}}\bar{r}_{i}\mathscr{D}^{\lambda}_{i}(S)
is equal to the optimal objective value of the linear optimization problem (
84
), the second equality follows from algebra, and the third equality follows from the fact that
\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}}g_{m,i,\kappa}=1
is one of the constraints in the linear optimization problem (
6
). Our proof of Claim
22
is thus complete.

\halmos

To simplify our exposition, the remainder of the proof of Theorem
6.2
focuses on the case where
\eta=0
. Indeed, the reformulations of objective function of the optimization problem (
OO
) for the case of
\eta=0
can be readily extended to the case of
\eta>0
by introducing
\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n,M))
auxiliary decision variables and auxiliary linear constraints in the dual problem. In the case of
\eta=0
, we observe from Claim
22
that the linear optimization problem (
82
) can be rewritten equivalently as

\displaystyle\underset{f,g}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}

(
82
)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}g_{m,i,\kappa}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}=0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}},

where the definition of the set
\mathfrak{N}(S)
can be found in the beginning of Appendix
I.4
.
It remains for us to show that the constraints of the linear optimization problem (
82
) can be reformulated using linear constraints and mixed-integer decision variables. Indeed, let
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
be a binary decision variable for each product
i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
that satisfies
x_{i}=1
if and only if product
i
is in the assortment
S
. Under this equivalence between an assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
and the binary decision variables
x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
, it follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{N}(\cdot)
that the following equality holds for all assortments
S\in\mathcal{S}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}(S)\triangleq\left\{(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:\begin{gathered}\left[x_{i}=1\textnormal{ and }i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}\right]\\
\textnormal{ or }\left[x_{i}=1\textnormal{ and }i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}\right]\\
\textnormal{ or }\left[\textnormal{there exists }j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ such that }\kappa=r_{j}\textnormal{ and }x_{j}=0\right]\end{gathered}\right\}.

Hence, it is easy to verify that the constraints in the linear optimization problem (
82
) of the form

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}=0\quad\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{N}(S)

will be satisfied if and only if

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq x_{j}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}\textnormal{ and }j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}:\kappa=r_{j}.

We thus conclude from Claim
22
that the best-case expected revenue
\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
for each assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
is equal to the optimal objective value of the following linear optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{f,g}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}

(85)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}g_{m,i,\kappa}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq x_{j}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}\textnormal{ and }j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}:\kappa=r_{j}

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}.

Using the linear optimization problem (
85
) to reformulate the objective function of (
OO
), we conclude for the case of
\eta=0
that the optimization problem (
OO
) is equivalent to the following mixed-integer optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{f,g,x}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}

(86)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}g_{m,i,\kappa}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq x_{j}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}\textnormal{ and }j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}:\kappa=r_{j}

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}

\displaystyle x_{0}=1

\displaystyle x_{i}\in\{0,1\}

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}.

Our proof of Theorem
6.2
is thus complete.
\halmos

J.3
Proof of Theorem
6.3

Let Assumption
5.3
hold, let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{M}\in\mathcal{S}
, and let
\theta
be any constant that satisfies
\theta\leq\max_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
.
22
22

22

It is easy to see that the requirement that
\theta\leq\max_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)
is a necessary and sufficient condition for the optimization problem (
PO
) to have a feasible solution.
For any assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
, let
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
be a binary decision variable for each product
i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
that satisfies
x_{i}=1
if and only if product
i
is in the assortment
S
. Under this equivalence between an assortment
S\in\mathcal{S}
and the binary decision variables
x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
, it follows immediately from the proof of Theorem
5.12
that the set
\left\{S\in\mathcal{S}:\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)\geq\theta\right\}

can be reformulated using the binary decision variables
x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
and an addition of
\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n,M))
auxiliary decision variables and
\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n,M))
linear constraints. Moreover, it follows immediately from the proof of Theorem
6.2
that the level set
\left\{(S,t)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathbb{R}:\max_{\lambda\in\mathcal{U}}\mathscr{R}^{\lambda}(S)\geq t\right\}
can be reformulated using the binary decision variables
x\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
and an addition of
\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n,M))
auxiliary decision variables and
\mathcal{O}(\textnormal{poly}(n,M))
linear constraints. We thus obtain the following mixed-integer optimization reformulation of (
PO
):

\displaystyle\underset{f,g,\alpha,\beta,\gamma,x}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i,\kappa:(M,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}\kappa g_{M,i,\kappa}

(87)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{\kappa:(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}}g_{m,i,\kappa}=v_{m,i}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}:((m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}),(m,i,\kappa))\in{\mathfrak{E}}}f_{m-1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime},i,\kappa}=g_{m,i,\kappa}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}:m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq 1-x_{i}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}:i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\left\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\right\}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\leq x_{j}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{V}\textnormal{ and }j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}:\kappa=r_{j}

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M},i\in S_{m}}v_{m,i}\alpha_{m,i}\geq\theta

\displaystyle\alpha_{m,i}-\beta_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}\right\}-\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\mathbb{I}\left\{m\in\{2,\ldots,M\}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa\mathbb{I}\left\{m=M\right\}

\displaystyle+r_{n}x_{i}\mathbb{I}\left\{i\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\neq r_{i}\right\}

\displaystyle+r_{n}x_{i}\mathbb{I}\left\{i\notin\mathcal{B}_{m}\textnormal{ and }\kappa\in\{r_{j}:j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\}\right\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}_{m}}\mathbb{I}\left\{\kappa=r_{j}\right\}r_{n}(1-x_{j})

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}

\displaystyle\beta_{m,i,\kappa}+\gamma_{m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\leq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle x_{0}=1

\displaystyle\alpha_{m,i}\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\;i\in S_{m}

\displaystyle\beta_{m,i,\kappa},\gamma_{m,i,\kappa}\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}

\displaystyle x_{i}\in\{0,1\}

\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{N}_{0}

\displaystyle f_{m,i,\kappa,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall((m,i,\kappa),(m+1,i^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime}))\in{\mathfrak{E}}

\displaystyle g_{m,i,\kappa}\geq 0

\displaystyle\forall(m,i,\kappa)\in{\mathfrak{V}}.

Our proof of Theorem
6.3
is thus complete.
\halmos
